		<item>
		<title>Push-based KV transfer to improve TTFT and TPOT for disaggregated inference in vLLM</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/push-based-kv-transfer-to-improve-ttft-and-tpot-for-disaggregated-inference-in-vllm/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Sunita Nadampalli]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 19 Oct 2026 16:05:17 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">3be758421ce5b5c9a23f149082c2bc420bfa9905</guid>

					<description>vLLM's prefill-decode disaggregation splits LLM inference across engine instances but must transfer the KV cache between them. This post introduces a push-based NIXL connector that moves transfer coordination off the critical path, delivering up to 3x better TTFT and 30% better TPOT in benchmarks on Amazon EC2 P5en instances with AWS EFA.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;vLLM&lt;/a&gt; is an open-source inference and serving engine for large language models (LLMs). LLM inference has two distinct phases: prefill, which processes the input prompt and builds the key-value (KV) cache, and decode, which uses that cache to generate output one token at a time. These phases have different compute, memory, and scaling characteristics. &lt;a href="https://docs.vllm.ai/en/latest/features/disagg_prefill/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Prefill-decode (P-D) disaggregation&lt;/a&gt; runs them on separate engine instances, allowing each phase to scale and be optimized independently. This can improve resource utilization, throughput, and latency under load. However, it also requires transferring the KV cache from the prefill instance to the decode instance before token generation can begin, making efficient KV transfer critical to time-to-first-token (TTFT).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;One of the ways vLLM performs this transfer is through the NVIDIA Inference Xfer Library (NIXL) &lt;a href="https://docs.vllm.ai/en/latest/features/nixl_connector_usage/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;connector&lt;/a&gt;, which moves KV blocks over interconnects that support Remote Direct Memory Access (RDMA), such as AWS Elastic Fabric Adapter (&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/efa.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS EFA&lt;/a&gt;, the low-latency RDMA network used across servers in AWS). On &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/efa.html#efa-instance-types" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;supported instance types&lt;/a&gt;, AWS EFA supports GPUDirect RDMA and Neuron Direct RDMA. NIXL can therefore transfer KV blocks directly between GPU or &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/machine-learning/trainium/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Trainium&lt;/a&gt; device memory without staging through host memory. The default mode for NIXL connector is &lt;strong&gt;pull-based&lt;/strong&gt;: After the prefill instance finishes computing the prompt KV cache, the router relays the source metadata, including the prefill block IDs, to the decode instance. The decode instance then allocates destination blocks, prepares the NIXL transfer, and initiates NIXL READ to pull the KV blocks from the prefill instance. Because these coordination and setup steps occur sequentially after prefill and before decoding can begin, they extend the TTFT critical path, as shown in the following timeline.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21812-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21812-1.png" alt="Pull-mode timeline: router relay, decode-side allocation, and NIXL READ run serially on the path to first token." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Pull-mode KV transfer timeline, where the router relay, decode-side allocation, and NIXL READ run serially on the path to the first token&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This post introduces a &lt;strong&gt;push-based&lt;/strong&gt; NIXL connector that moves post-prefill transfer coordination and setup off the critical path. If you run disaggregated prefill/decode inference with vLLM, push mode can improve application responsiveness by reducing TTFT and TPOT, particularly at high request rates.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In our benchmark on a 1P1D deployment using Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) P5en instances connected with EFA, push mode delivered up to 3x better TTFT and up to 30 percent better time-per-output-token (TPOT) compared to pull mode (see the Performance data section) for the Qwen3-32B model. Results depend on workload and configuration.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;We built and validated the feature on AWS Trainium instance clusters connected with EFA, and then we contributed it to vLLM as an accelerator-agnostic feature. The results here are from an Amazon EC2 P5en (GPU) cluster with EFA, showing the gains carry over to GPUs. This post explains the push-based architecture, transfer flow, threading model, performance results, and how to reproduce the benchmark.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="push-based-kv-transfer"&gt;Push-based KV transfer&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This section explains the push-based design: its architecture, the transfer protocol, and the threading model that keeps KV transfer off the critical path.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="architecture-overview"&gt;Architecture overview&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Push-based transfer improves TTFT and decode throughput for two reasons:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;It removes the serialization of the pull model&lt;/strong&gt; by overlapping producer and consumer work. The decode instance registers its target blocks in advance while the producer is still computing KV. The transfer then begins as soon as the producer’s blocks are ready, rather than after a consumer-side ID-discovery round trip, removing that round trip from the path to first token.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;It moves the transfer-setup time off the decode instance.&lt;/strong&gt; That setup runs on the prefill side and on a background thread. It prepares the NIXL descriptors and submits the transfer, so the decode instance does not spend those cycles during its latency-critical token-generation phase.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The net effect is lower TTFT and higher output-token throughput on the decode instance.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The push connector coordinates three actors: the decode instance registers its target blocks up front, the prefill instance matches each request and issues the transfer, and a dedicated background thread carries the KV write off the engine’s forward-pass loop. The following diagram shows how these pieces fit together.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21812-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21812-2.png" alt="Push-based KV transfer architecture overview" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Push-based KV transfer architecture, showing how the decode instance, prefill instance, and background transfer thread coordinate&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="implementation-details"&gt;Implementation details&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The protocol has three logical steps.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;1. Registration (decode side).&lt;/strong&gt; When the decode instance allocates local blocks for a request requiring remote prefilling, it prepares a registration describing where the prefill instance should write the KV: its own identity and the block locations it has just reserved. Within the same engine step, the worker transmits this registration to the prefill instance as a NIXL notification. Because registration happens at allocation time, the connector knows the destination blocks before the prefill instance completes prefill.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;2. Rendezvous (prefill side).&lt;/strong&gt; The registration from the decoder and the completion of prefill on the producer can arrive in either order, and the connector handles both. If the registration arrives first, the producer holds it until its own prefill finishes. If prefill finishes first, the producer holds the request’s blocks until the registration arrives. Whichever event happens second triggers the transfer. The connector pairs requests by ID, and the matching accounts for reissued requests, correctly associating preempted or retried requests.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;3. Transfer (prefill side).&lt;/strong&gt; Once a request is matched, the producer establishes a connection to the decode instance (if one does not already exist) and issues an NIXL WRITE that copies the KV blocks directly into the decoder’s registered memory, addressing each destination tensor-parallel rank. The producer and consumer may use different internal block layouts. Each side maps block locations to its own physical layout at transfer time, allowing different block sizes on each side. Each WRITE carries a completion notification that NIXL delivers to the decode instance once the data arrives. The decoder counts these notifications, one per producer rank that writes into it. It treats the KV as received only after all expected notifications have arrived, at which point the request proceeds to decode.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="threading-model"&gt;Threading model&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The push connector separates model execution from KV-transfer preparation by using a dedicated background transfer thread on each prefill worker. At the start of a request, the decode main loop allocates its destination KV blocks and registers them with the prefill instance while the prefill main loop computes the prompt KV cache. After both the destination registration and prompt KV are ready, the prefill main loop hands the KV blocks to the background thread and quickly returns to model execution.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The background thread establishes or reuses the NIXL connection, maps the request’s KV block IDs to memory descriptors, merges contiguous blocks into larger transfer regions, and submits the NIXL WRITE to the decode instance. When NIXL completes the transfer, it sends a completion notification to the decode instance, which then begins token generation. Keeping descriptor preparation and transfer submission off the latency-critical main loops prevents this per-request work from delaying model execution. The following diagram shows these main-loop and background-thread handoffs for a single request.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21812-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21812-3.png" alt="Threading model showing the decode and prefill main loops handing KV blocks to a background thread that performs the NIXL transfer off the forward-pass loop" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Main-loop and background-thread handoffs for a single request in the push-based threading model&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="timing-comparison-of-pull-and-push"&gt;Timing comparison of pull and push&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram summarizes the pull and push transfer flows described in the preceding sections and highlights their effect on TTFT. Although both modes use the same NIXL and EFA/RDMA data path, pull performs the metadata relay and decode-side setup after prefill, while push overlaps decode-side setup with prefill. As a result, push removes these operations from the post-prefill critical path, allowing decoding to begin sooner.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21812-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21812-4.png" alt="Side-by-side timeline comparing pull and push KV transfer, showing push overlapping decode-side setup with prefill to shorten the path to the first token" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Pull and push transfer flows compared, highlighting how push overlaps decode-side setup with prefill&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The two modes also involve a routing tradeoff. Pull mode allows the router to select a decode instance after prefill completes, which provides greater flexibility for load-aware routing and quality-of-service management. Push mode selects the decode instance earlier so that destination setup can overlap with prefill. In return, under comparable load, the prefill instance can release its KV blocks sooner because the transfer starts as soon as both the prompt KV and destination registration are ready.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="performance-data"&gt;Performance data&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For evaluation, we used Qwen3-32B model on a 1P1D deployment using Amazon EC2 P5en instances connected with EFA. To isolate P→D transfer performance, we configured the benchmark to disable prefix caching with &lt;code&gt;--no-enable-prefix-caching&lt;/code&gt;. This configuration makes the decode node retrieve the full KV cache from the prefill node for every request. Otherwise, overlapping prompts could reuse the decode node’s local cache and bypass the transfer, which would produce misleading comparison results.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following chart compares TTFT for push and pull KV transfer. We sweep input lengths 1k–16k and request rates of 1–8 queries per second (QPS). At low load and short prompts, the two modes perform almost identically. As QPS and input length grow, pull mode degrades sharply, reaching about 1,744 ms at 8 QPS and 16k. Push mode stays far lower at roughly 540 ms, around 3x reduction. The gap opens because pull mode posts NIXL descriptors inline on the decode worker, synchronously within its engine loop, so that work stalls token generation. Push mode moves it to a background thread, off the critical path.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21812-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img title="Push versus pull TTFT benchmark" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21812-5.png" alt="Four-panel TTFT comparison of push and pull KV transfer for Qwen3-32B on Amazon EC2 P5en instances with EFA. Test configuration: 1P1D topology, 128 output tokens, and prefix caching disabled on the Decode node." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: TTFT for push compared to pull KV transfer across input lengths and request rates (1P1D topology, 128 output tokens, prefix caching disabled on the decode node)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This chart shows TPOT under the same conditions. The pattern mirrors TTFT. At light load, push and pull are close, around 5.6–5.8 ms. As load and input length rise, pull climbs to about 10.4 ms at 4–8 QPS and 16k, while push holds near 7 ms. The reason is that push offloads descriptor preparation and transfer submission from the decode worker. Token generation is not starved, so per-token latency stays flat even at high concurrency and long contexts.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21812-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img title="Push versus pull TPOT benchmark" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21812-6.png" alt="Four-panel TPOT comparison of push and pull KV transfer for Qwen3-32B on Amazon EC2 P5en instances with EFA. Test configuration: 1P1D topology, 128 output tokens, and prefix caching disabled on the Decode node." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: TPOT for push compared to pull KV transfer across input lengths and request rates (1P1D topology, 128 output tokens, prefix caching disabled on the decode node)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="how-to-run-benchmark"&gt;How to run benchmark&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To reproduce our 1P1D benchmark configuration, one prefill node and one decode node, using Amazon EC2 P5en instances connected with EFA, refer to the detailed instructions in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm-project.github.io/blob/main/_posts/2026-10-14-nixl-push-connector.md#appendix-reproducing-our-results" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Reproducing our results&lt;/a&gt; guide. The guide covers environment setup, launching the vLLM prefill and decode servers, running the benchmark, and collecting results. The feature can run on other supported configurations, but performance results will vary depending on the hardware, model, workload, and network configuration.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how the push-based KV connector overlaps prefill computation with decode-side block allocation and registration, removing the post-prefill router round trip and decode-side transfer setup from the critical path. This reduces TTFT and TPOT, particularly at high request rates. In our Qwen3-32B benchmark on a 1P1D deployment using Amazon EC2 P5en instances connected with EFA, push mode provided up to a 3x improvement in TTFT and 30 percent improvement in TPOT compared with pull mode. Results vary by workload and configuration. To use push mode, configure both the prefill and decode servers to use &lt;code&gt;NixlPushConnector&lt;/code&gt; instead of &lt;code&gt;NixlConnector&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Choose the push-based connector when early decode node selection is acceptable and lower TTFT is the priority. The pull-based connector remains the better fit for workloads that require &lt;a href="https://vllm.ai/blog/2026-10-12-bidirectional-kvxfer-multiturn-agentic-workload" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;bidirectional KV transfer&lt;/a&gt; or defer decode-instance selection until after prefill for load-aware routing. vLLM supports both push- and pull-based KV connectors, so you can select the approach that best fits your deployment.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;As a future improvement, we are exploring bringing the background NIXL threading model to the pull-mode connector, moving NIXL setup off the critical path so that pull-mode workloads could benefit from the same optimizations. We have opened a &lt;a href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/45211" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;pull request&lt;/a&gt; to explore this.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="related-resources"&gt;Related resources&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.vllm.ai/en/latest/features/disagg_prefill.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;vLLM disaggregated serving guide&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/efa.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS EFA&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.vllm.ai/en/latest/design/nixl_kv_push_connector/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Push-mode NIXL KV connector&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;

&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Improve TTFT for multi-turn agentic workloads with bi-directional KV transfer in vLLM</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/improve-ttft-for-multi-turn-agentic-workloads-with-bi-directional-kv-transfer-in-vllm/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Sunita Nadampalli]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 19 Oct 2026 16:03:23 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">02a1dd975b2fff9f32ce315def712a434de65bee</guid>

					<description>Multi-turn agentic workloads reprocess growing conversation context each turn, driving up latency. See how bi-directional KV cache transfer lets prefill and decode nodes reuse previously computed context across turns, cutting time to first token by up to 1.6x for Qwen3-32B and 3.3x for GLM-5-FP8 on Amazon EC2 P5en instances with EFA.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Multi-turn agentic workloads build on accumulated context as an agent reasons, invokes tools, and incorporates earlier results. With each new turn, the client resends the conversation history, including system instructions, prior prompts, tool results, and model responses, causing the growing context to be processed repeatedly. In &lt;a href="https://docs.vllm.ai/en/latest/features/disagg_prefill/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Prefill-decode (P-D) disaggregation&lt;/a&gt;, each request is split across specialized nodes: the prefill node processes the prompt and builds its key-value (KV) cache, then the decode node retrieves those KV blocks and generates the response.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Conventional disaggregated serving, however, only transfers KV cache in one direction: from prefill to decode node. This one-directional flow creates two inefficiencies for multi-turn workloads. First, after generating a response, the decode node holds KV cache entries for both the prompt and the newly generated tokens. When processing the next turn, the prefill node cannot retrieve those entries, so it must recompute the KV projections for the previous response tokens. Second, the decode node maintains the session state while the prefill nodes don’t, increasing the probability of cache eviction for active sessions on the prefill nodes. On a full cache miss, the prefill node must recompute KV projections for the entire conversation history, including system instructions, prior user requests, tool results, and model-generated responses. As the conversation grows, this redundant work consumes more accelerator capacity and increases time to first token (TTFT).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;When you build conversational assistants and agentic applications, longer interaction histories can increase latency and consume accelerator capacity through repeated prefill computation. In this post, we show how &lt;strong&gt;bi-directional KV cache transfer&lt;/strong&gt; lets prefill and decode nodes reuse previously computed context across turns, which improves accelerator utilization and helps applications sustain responsive TTFT as conversations grow.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In our multi-turn benchmarks on a 1P1D deployment using Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) P5en instances, bi-directional KV transfer reduced TTFT by up to 1.6x for Qwen3-32B and up to 3.3x for zai-org/GLM-5-FP8 (see the Performance data section). Results vary with workload and configuration. The instances are connected with AWS Elastic Fabric Adapter (&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/efa.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS EFA&lt;/a&gt;), a low-latency remote direct memory access (RDMA) network used across servers in AWS. On &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/efa.html#efa-instance-types" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;supported instance types&lt;/a&gt;, AWS EFA supports GPUDirect RDMA and Neuron Direct RDMA to move KV blocks directly between GPU or AWS Trainium device memory on the prefill and decode nodes.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;We originally built and validated the feature on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/machine-learning/trainium/"&gt;AWS Trainium&lt;/a&gt; instance clusters connected with EFA and contributed it to vLLM as an accelerator-agnostic capability. The results presented here are from an Amazon EC2 P5en GPU cluster with EFA, showing that the benefits also carry over to GPUs. This post explains the bi-directional KV transfer architecture, router metadata flow, KV-block retention and recompute controls, performance results, and how to reproduce the benchmark.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="bi-directional-kv-transfer"&gt;Bi-directional KV transfer&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Bi-directional KV transfer is a mechanism in which a decode node returns previously computed KV to a prefill node on subsequent turns of a conversation. This avoids recomputing shared context on the prefill node and is governed by a configurable KV recompute threshold. It mitigates both computational-redundancy scenarios described earlier. To make it work, the KV must survive past the turn that produced it and travel in the reverse direction, coordinated by a KV-cache-aware router.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="nixl-kv-connector-enhancements-for-bi-directional-transfer"&gt;NIXL KV connector enhancements for bi-directional transfer&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;We extended the NVIDIA Inference Xfer Library (NIXL) &lt;a href="https://docs.vllm.ai/en/latest/features/nixl_connector_usage/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;KV connector&lt;/a&gt; to let prefill nodes consume remote KV blocks, similar to how decode nodes already consume KV blocks that the prefill node provides. This is not a trivial change, because the two node types are architecturally different: prefill nodes compute KV representations for new prompt tokens and can provide complete prompt KV blocks. Decode nodes lack the capability (or efficiency) to generate complete KV representations and can only serve previously cached KV blocks. As a result, prefill and decode nodes still carry their own roles and logic for computing the metadata of remote and local KV blocks. The actual KV transfer mechanism (a NIXL READ), however, is common to both the &lt;code&gt;P-&amp;gt;D&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;D-&amp;gt;P&lt;/code&gt; directions.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram first shows the existing flow in blue. The router sends the request to prefill node, prefill node computes the prompt KV and returns its block metadata, and the router forwards the request and metadata to decode node for the standard P→D NIXL READ. The numbered red path illustrates the bi-directional KV transfer additions: (1) decode node retains eligible KV blocks after generation, (2) returns their metadata in the streaming response, and (3) the router caches that metadata for the next prefill request. Prefill node then (4) checks the remotely reusable KV after it applies local cache matches, (5) queues an asynchronous receive when the reusable token count meets the threshold, and (6) performs a D→P NIXL READ before computing the remaining suffix.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21811-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21811-1.png" alt="Flow diagram contrasting the standard prefill-to-decode KV path (blue) with the bi-directional additions numbered one through six (red)" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Standard prefill-to-decode flow (blue) and the bi-directional KV transfer additions (red)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="kv-cache-aware-router"&gt;KV-cache-aware router&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;vLLM includes connector-side support for bi-directional KV transfer, while coordinating KV reuse across conversation turns requires a KV-cache-aware router. vLLM GitHub provides an &lt;a href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/blob/main/examples/disaggregated/disaggregated_serving/disagg_proxy_multiturn.py" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;example proxy&lt;/a&gt; that implements the flow used in this post which can be adapted for your environment.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The KV-cache-aware router is a lightweight, stateful control-plane component between the client, prefill, and decode nodes. It maintains an in-memory mapping from the client-provided &lt;code&gt;conversation_id&lt;/code&gt; to decode’s &lt;code&gt;kv_transfer_params&lt;/code&gt;. The router stores only block-location metadata, not KV tensors, and obtains that metadata from the response stream rather than separately querying decode node.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows how the router manages KV metadata across conversation turns. On the first turn, the cache lookup misses and the request follows the standard P→D flow. The router then saves decode’s metadata from the response stream. On later turns, the router retrieves that metadata using the &lt;code&gt;conversation_id&lt;/code&gt; so prefill can reuse KV from decode. It then refreshes the metadata after generation for the next turn.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21811-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img title="KV-cache-aware router metadata flow across turns" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21811-2.png" alt="Two stacked sequence diagrams focused on the KV-cache-aware router. On turn one, the router records a cache miss, sends the request to Prefill, receives Prefill metadata, sends Decode the request and Prefill metadata, saves Decode metadata from the stream, and returns the response. On a later turn, the router pops saved Decode metadata, sends it to Prefill, receives updated Prefill metadata, sends Decode the request and Prefill metadata, refreshes the Decode metadata entry, and returns the response. Purple arrows show the Decode-to-Prefill and Prefill-to-Decode KV reads; KV tensors do not pass through the router." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: KV-cache-aware router metadata flow across conversation turns&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="block-retention-ttl"&gt;Block retention TTL&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Normally a decode instance frees a request’s blocks as soon as generation finishes. Under bi-directional KV transfer mode, it holds them for a configurable lifetime (&lt;code&gt;decoder_kv_blocks_ttl&lt;/code&gt;, default 480 seconds) and publishes their location and expiry. The conversation’s KV, both prompt prefix and generated response, then stays available for the next turn. For a turn that arrives before the time to live (TTL) expires, prefill node reuses the KV. One that arrives later falls back to normal prefill. Because this TTL is fixed and not renewed, tune it to the expected time between turns to balance KV reuse against accelerator-memory usage. A natural next improvement is to extend the heartbeat-renewed lease to the decode side. A decode instance could then reclaim blocks as soon as a conversation goes idle rather than at the end of a fixed timer.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="kv-recompute-threshold"&gt;KV recompute threshold&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;KV reuse isn’t always faster than local recomputation. For a short reusable prefix, interconnect and coordination overhead can exceed the time needed to recompute the tokens. The &lt;code&gt;kv_recompute_threshold&lt;/code&gt; setting, which defaults to 64 tokens, controls this decision. Prefill node recomputes when the token count to compute is below the threshold and pulls KV from decode node when it is at or above the threshold. Deployments can tune this value based on model computation time, KV size, and interconnect performance.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="performance-data"&gt;Performance data&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For evaluation, we used Qwen3-32B and GLM-5-FP8 models on a 1P1D deployment using Amazon EC2 P5en instances connected with EFA. To emulate scenarios in which the prefill node has evicted its local cache or the router selects a different prefill node that does not have the conversation’s KV blocks, we disabled prefix caching on prefill node with &lt;code&gt;--no-enable-prefix-caching&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Across both the models, turning on bi-directional KV transfer reduces prefill computation on multi-turn conversations, and the benefit typically grows with prompt length. With the feature OFF, the prefill instance recomputes the entire grown context each turn, so TTFT rises steeply. Qwen3-32B climbs from 158 ms at 2k to 778 ms at 24k, and GLM-5-FP8 from 500 ms at 2k to 2,334 ms at 20k. With it ON, the decode instance’s retained KV is reused instead of recomputed, keeping TTFT far flatter. Qwen3-32B rises only from 122 ms to 460 ms over the same range (about a 1.6x reduction at the longest prompt), while GLM-5-FP8 rises from 358 ms to 702 ms and runs up to 3.3x below the recompute path at long prompts. The prefill-duration breakdown explains why.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Bi-directional transfer adds a small KV-transfer time (about 30 ms to 130 ms for Qwen3-32B; about 29 ms to 110 ms for GLM-5-FP8), but the prefill compute it removes far outweighs that KV-transfer time. As a result, the total prefill duration with transfer ON (Qwen3-32B: 128 ms to 414 ms; GLM-5-FP8: 316 ms to 541 ms) stays well below the recompute-only path (Qwen3-32B: 153 ms to 744 ms; GLM-5-FP8: 500 ms to 2,331 ms). The larger GLM-5-FP8 model shows a bigger absolute and relative gap, since its higher per-token recompute time makes KV reuse pay off even more as models and contexts scale.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="qwen3-32b"&gt;Qwen3-32B&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following chart plots TTFT against prompt length for Qwen3-32B, comparing bi-directional KV transfer ON versus OFF.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21811-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21811-3.png" alt="Line chart of Qwen3-32B time to first token versus prompt length with bi-directional KV transfer on and off" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Qwen3-32B TTFT versus prompt length with bi-directional KV transfer on and off&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The next chart breaks the prefill duration for the same runs into its recompute and KV-transfer components.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21811-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21811-4.png" alt="Bar chart breaking Qwen3-32B prefill duration into recompute and KV-transfer components" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Qwen3-32B prefill duration split into recompute and KV-transfer components&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Together the charts show TTFT staying nearly flat with the feature ON while the OFF path climbs steeply as context grows, because the small KV-transfer time is far outweighed by the recompute it removes.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="zai-orgglm-5-fp8"&gt;zai-org/GLM-5-FP8&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The same two views follow for GLM-5-FP8. The first plots TTFT against prompt length with the feature ON versus OFF.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21811-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img title="zai-org/GLM-5-FP8 TTFT with KV reuse on and off" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21811-5.png" alt="Line plot for zai-org/GLM-5-FP8 on Amazon EC2 P5en instances with AWS EFA, comparing time to first token with bi-directional KV transfer enabled and disabled across input prompt lengths." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: zai-org/GLM-5-FP8 TTFT with KV reuse on and off&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The second breaks the prefill duration into its recompute and KV-transfer components.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21811-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img title="zai-org/GLM-5-FP8 prefill duration breakdown" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-21811-6.png" alt="Grouped bar plot for zai-org/GLM-5-FP8 on Amazon EC2 P5en instances with AWS EFA, comparing prefill duration with bi-directional KV transfer enabled and disabled and showing the KV-transfer component." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: zai-org/GLM-5-FP8 prefill duration breakdown&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;GLM-5-FP8 shows a much larger gap than Qwen3-32B, up to about 3.3x lower TTFT at long prompts, because its higher per-token recompute time makes KV reuse pay off even more as context length grows.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="how-to-run-benchmark"&gt;How to run benchmark&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To reproduce our 1P1D benchmark configuration, one prefill node and one decode node, using Amazon EC2 P5en instances with EFA, follow the step-by-step instructions in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm-project.github.io/blob/main/_posts/2026-10-12-bidirectional-kvxfer-multiturn-agentic-workload.md#appendix-reproducing-our-results" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Reproducing our results guide&lt;/a&gt;. The guide covers environment setup, launching the vLLM prefill and decode servers, running the benchmark, and collecting results. The feature can run on other supported configurations, but performance results will vary depending on the hardware, model, workload, and network configuration.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how bi-directional KV cache transfer can reduce TTFT as an agent’s conversation history grows. In P-D disaggregated deployments, the feature lets prefill node reuse KV blocks retained by decode node across turns, reducing repeated computation. Configurable block-retention and recompute thresholds determine when reuse occurs. On a 1P1D deployment using Amazon EC2 P5en instances connected with EFA, at the longest prompt lengths tested, we observed TTFT reductions of 1.6x for Qwen3-32B and 3.3x for zai-org/GLM-5-FP8. Results depend on the workload and deployment configuration. To implement this in your environment, use vLLM v0.21.0 or later with NixlConnector, enable &lt;code&gt;bidirectional_kv_xfer&lt;/code&gt; on both prefill and decode servers, use a kv-cache-aware router, and follow the &lt;a href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm-project.github.io/blob/main/_posts/2026-10-12-bidirectional-kvxfer-multiturn-agentic-workload.md#appendix-reproducing-our-results" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub reproduction guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="related-resources"&gt;Related resources&lt;/h2&gt;

 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/efa.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS EFA&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.vllm.ai/en/latest/features/disagg_prefill/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Prefill-Decode (P-D) disaggregated serving&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
