			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Introducing Reflection Beam: an open-weight model for agentic coding on AWS</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-reflection-beam-an-open-weight-model-for-agentic-coding-on-aws/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Clement Perrot]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 16 Oct 2026 15:23:10 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker HyperPod]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker JumpStart]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">cdaa150f662455f5a5b50cfecf81b2cdf715e1e7</guid>

					<description>Reflection AI's Beam is an open-weight model for agentic coding and long-horizon tasks. This post shows how to deploy Beam on AWS: start with an Amazon SageMaker JumpStart real-time endpoint, or use Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Inference with JumpStart, SGLang, or vLLM for Kubernetes-native control over distributed GPU infrastructure.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://reflection.ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Reflection AI&lt;/a&gt; introduced Beam, an open-weight model for agentic coding and long-horizon tasks such as maintaining code, using tools, interpreting results, and completing multi-step workflows. The open-weight release gives developers and researchers control over the runtime, deployment environment, and customization process.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we explain Reflection’s vision and show how you can deploy Beam on AWS. Start with an Amazon SageMaker JumpStart real-time endpoint for a fully managed path. When you need Kubernetes-native control over distributed GPU infrastructure, use &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-model-deployment.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Inference&lt;/a&gt; with JumpStart, SGLang, or vLLM. We also explain how Beam’s 501 billion total parameters shape checkpoint storage, weight memory, sharding, and parallelism, while its 23 billion active parameters influence per-token compute.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;At launch, Beam is available in Amazon SageMaker JumpStart in US East (N. Virginia) and US East (Ohio).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="reflections-vision-for-open-intelligence"&gt;Reflection’s vision for open intelligence&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Reflection’s mission is to &lt;a href="https://reflection.ai/about" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;make intelligence open and accessible to all&lt;/a&gt; through open model weights, published research, and open source software. Its &lt;a href="https://reflection.ai/solutions" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;full AI stack&lt;/a&gt; spans models, core software, production infrastructure, and applications. Beam brings this approach to developers building software engineering and agentic systems.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="about-reflection-beam"&gt;About Reflection Beam&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Beam is a sparse mixture-of-experts model for resolving software issues, modifying code, calling tools, and acting on intermediate results. It has 501 billion total parameters and 23 billion active parameters, and Reflection reports an effective context length of 1 million tokens.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Reflection releases Beam’s weights through Hugging Face under the Apache 2.0 license. Beam 1.0 is available in BF16, FP8, and NVFP4 checkpoint variants. These formats provide different memory footprints and hardware compatibility. You can deploy the model with the AWS managed JumpStart container configuration or inspect and customize the model with SGLang or vLLM in your AWS environment.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="understand-the-deployment-architecture"&gt;Understand the deployment architecture&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Choose the serving path that meets your control and scaling requirements.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Start with a &lt;strong&gt;SageMaker JumpStart real-time endpoint&lt;/strong&gt; when you want an AWS managed model package, endpoint, and invocation API without operating Kubernetes.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Use &lt;strong&gt;JumpStart on HyperPod Inference&lt;/strong&gt; when you want the AWS managed container and model configuration on a HyperPod cluster orchestrated by Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Use &lt;strong&gt;SGLang or vLLM on HyperPod Inference&lt;/strong&gt; when you need to control the serving image, runtime arguments, quantization, or distributed execution.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Size the deployment for checkpoint storage, weight memory, KV cache, context length, batching, runtime overhead, and the supported parallelism.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Define the expected concurrency and latency targets, then configure replicas, caching, routing, and autoscaling from measured workload behavior.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The JumpStart real-time path packages the model, container, and endpoint behind a managed deployment workflow. The HyperPod path exposes the cluster and serving runtime so that you can control distributed execution, caching, routing, and scaling.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the components used by the HyperPod path. Beam can be sourced through SageMaker JumpStart, Hugging Face Hub, or an optional Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) mirror. The distributed workers use the AWS managed JumpStart container or a custom SGLang or vLLM runtime.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-22133-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-22133-1-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram showing Beam sourced from SageMaker JumpStart, Hugging Face Hub, or an optional Amazon S3 mirror into Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Inference, with the Inference Operator managing local model and image caching and the Inference Gateway and Endpoint Picker routing requests to distributed JumpStart, SGLang, or vLLM workers monitored by Amazon CloudWatch." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Distributed Beam deployment with Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Inference&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;You need:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with permissions for your selected SageMaker deployment path and its supporting AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM), networking, and storage resources.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Access to Beam through SageMaker JumpStart or one of the Beam 1.0 checkpoint repositories on Hugging Face.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Acceptance of the model terms, the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI), and Python 3.11 or later.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;GPU quota and capacity for a supported Beam configuration.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="plan-and-obtain-gpu-capacity"&gt;Plan and obtain GPU capacity&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Beam’s 501 billion total parameters require a multi-GPU deployment. BF16 has the largest weight-memory requirement, so plan a multi-node configuration with enough aggregate GPU memory for the weights and runtime overhead. For FP8, use &lt;code&gt;ml.p5.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; with NVIDIA H100 GPUs or &lt;code&gt;ml.p5e.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;ml.p5en.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; with H200 GPUs. For NVFP4, use &lt;code&gt;ml.p6-b200.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; with NVIDIA B200 GPUs. Confirm that the released checkpoint, runtime, and parallel layout support the selected instance type.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Calculate memory for weights, KV cache, and runtime overhead for one replica, then confirm the estimate with a load test.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Choose an AWS Region that supports the required accelerated instance.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Check the service quota and available capacity for the selected instance type. Use on-demand capacity when available, or use a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/reserve-capacity-with-training-plans.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker training plan&lt;/a&gt; as a short-term reservation for scheduled work.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Allow time for image transfer, weight download, shard loading, and runtime initialization.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;h2 id="path-1-get-started-with-a-sagemaker-jumpstart-real-time-endpoint"&gt;Path 1: Get started with a SageMaker JumpStart real-time endpoint&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Use a JumpStart real-time endpoint first when you want the shortest path from the model catalog to a managed HTTPS endpoint. The sample supports the BF16, FP8, and NVFP4 model IDs through one variant-aware command. Its README and Python file are the source of truth for supported instance types, request schema, deployment command, and cleanup command.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Clone the sample repository.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;git clone https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples.git&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Enter the SageMaker JumpStart deployment sample directory.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cd sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/01-models/ReflectionAI/Beam-1.0-501B-A23B-NVFP4/&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Create and activate a Python virtual environment.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python3 -m venv .venv
source .venv/bin/activate&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Install the sample dependency.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python -m pip install -r requirements.txt&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Run the bounded deployment, invocation, and cleanup lifecycle for FP8.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python jumpstart_real_time.py \
--variant fp8 \
--endpoint-name reflection-beam-fp8-smoke&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;
  &lt;p&gt;The command deploys the FP8 endpoint, sends a short request, prints the response, and cleans up the endpoint. Use &lt;code&gt;bf16&lt;/code&gt;&amp;nbsp;or &lt;code&gt;nvfp4&lt;/code&gt;&amp;nbsp;to select another checkpoint variant; NVFP4 is the default when you omit &lt;code&gt;--variant&lt;/code&gt;. Use the Python functions in the README when you need to keep an endpoint running.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;h2 id="path-2-deploy-with-sagemaker-hyperpod-inference"&gt;Path 2: Deploy with SageMaker HyperPod Inference&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Use HyperPod Inference when you need Kubernetes-native control over distributed GPU infrastructure. You can use the AWS managed JumpStart container configuration or deploy Beam with SGLang or vLLM. The &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/tree/main/SageMakerHyperpod/reflection-beam" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HyperPod Inference deployment sample README&lt;/a&gt; and its configuration files are the source of truth for released manifests, model and image identifiers, supported runtime versions, and commands.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Before you begin, configure &lt;code&gt;kubectl&lt;/code&gt; access to an EKS-orchestrated HyperPod cluster with the HyperPod Inference Operator and required add-ons. You also need the cluster virtual private cloud (VPC), subnets, security groups, access to the selected Beam and runtime artifacts, and local NVMe capacity if you enable model caching. Use an isolated Hugging Face access token if the selected repository requires authentication.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Deploy a pinned Beam revision from Hugging Face Hub for development and experimentation. For production deployments and model caching, copy the checkpoint to Amazon S3 or Amazon FSx. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-model-deployment-model-caching.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HyperPod model and image caching&lt;/a&gt; can preload weights onto node-local NVMe storage and pre-pull the serving image.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-model-deployment-inference-gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HyperPod Inference Gateway&lt;/a&gt; provides an OpenAI-compatible interface and routes requests across replicas. HyperPod Inference also supports &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-model-deployment-caching-routing.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;managed tiered KV caching and intelligent routing&lt;/a&gt;. These are among the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-sagemaker-inference-2026-year-to-date-launches-in-review/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Inference capabilities introduced in 2026&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The next diagram expands the model preparation and runtime request flow. When model caching is enabled, the Inference Operator warms eligible nodes with the selected weights and serving image. At runtime, the Inference Gateway and Endpoint Picker can direct each request to a suitable JumpStart-managed, SGLang, or vLLM replica.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-22133-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-22133-2-1.png" alt="Detailed HyperPod flow showing the Inference Gateway, Endpoint Picker, routing signals, Inference Operator, host-local model cache, and JumpStart-managed, SGLang, or vLLM replicas." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: HyperPod model caching and request routing&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="prepare-deployment-artifacts"&gt;Prepare deployment artifacts&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Use immutable inputs so that you can reproduce deployment and performance results.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Use the JumpStart model release recorded in the deployment sample.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Choose the BF16, FP8, or NVFP4 checkpoint for a custom runtime.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;For a custom runtime, keep the public Beam commit SHA and image digest in the sample release configuration.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Mirror the released image into a private Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) repository only when your cluster cannot pull the upstream image or your organization requires an approved registry.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Keep tokens, generated secrets, private model IDs, registry credentials, and unpublished results out of the repository.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;h3 id="choose-jumpstart-sglang-or-vllm"&gt;Choose a serving path&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Start with SageMaker JumpStart unless your workload requires a specific inference runtime or configuration.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Serving path&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use it when&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What you manage&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;You want an AWS-managed container and model configuration with less runtime maintenance.&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Select the Beam model version, supported instance type, endpoint configuration, and scaling policy.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;SGLang or vLLM&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;You need a specific inference runtime or configuration, such as custom runtime arguments, parsers, quantization, or parallelism.&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Pin the supported runtime image, model revision, parser configuration, and distributed execution settings.&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;h3 id="deploy-with-sagemaker-jumpstart"&gt;Deploy with SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;SageMaker JumpStart reduces container integration work while HyperPod retains control over cluster capacity, scaling, and observability.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;In SageMaker Studio, open the &lt;strong&gt;JumpStart model catalog&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;All public models&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Beam&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;SageMaker HyperPod&lt;/strong&gt; as the deployment target.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Select the cluster and Kubernetes namespace.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Enter an endpoint name, and choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Confirm the deployment status before invoking the endpoint with the released JumpStart request schema.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For an infrastructure-as-code workflow, use the released JumpStart configuration in the HyperPod sample. You can inspect the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/blob/main/SageMakerHyperpod/reflection-beam/reflection-beam-jumpstart.yaml" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;reflection-beam-jumpstart.yaml manifest template&lt;/a&gt; before configuring the deployment.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Clone the sample repository, and enter the HyperPod Beam directory.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;git clone https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples.git
cd sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/SageMakerHyperpod/reflection-beam/&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Create the local configuration files described in the README.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cp config/deployment.env.example config/deployment.env
cp config/release.env.example config/release.env&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Validate the JumpStart configuration before changing the cluster.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python hyperpod_tool.py \
  --config config/deployment.env \
  --config config/release.env \
  validate jumpstart&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Deploy the JumpStart configuration.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python hyperpod_tool.py \
  --config config/deployment.env \
  --config config/release.env \
  deploy jumpstart \
  --timeout-seconds 1800&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The helper renders and applies the released &lt;code&gt;JumpStartModel&lt;/code&gt; resource without writing populated release values to disk.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="deploy-with-sglang-or-vllm"&gt;Deploy with SGLang or vLLM&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Choose SGLang or vLLM when you need direct control over the serving runtime. Before configuring the deployment, inspect the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/blob/main/SageMakerHyperpod/reflection-beam/reflection-beam-sglang.yaml" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;reflection-beam-sglang.yaml manifest template&lt;/a&gt; or the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/blob/main/SageMakerHyperpod/reflection-beam/reflection-beam-vllm.yaml" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;reflection-beam-vllm.yaml manifest template&lt;/a&gt;. The sample README remains the source of truth for released values and commands.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Clone the sample repository.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;git clone https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples.git&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Enter the HyperPod Beam directory.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cd sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/SageMakerHyperpod/reflection-beam/&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Create the local configuration files described in the README.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cp config/deployment.env.example config/deployment.env
cp config/release.env.example config/release.env&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Validate the selected configuration before changing the cluster.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python hyperpod_tool.py \
  --config config/deployment.env \
  --config config/release.env \
  validate sglang&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Deploy Beam, and wait for a terminal deployment state.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python hyperpod_tool.py \
  --config config/deployment.env \
  --config config/release.env \
  deploy sglang \
  --timeout-seconds 1800&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;
  &lt;p&gt;Replace &lt;code&gt;sglang&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;vllm&lt;/code&gt; to use vLLM. The helper validates the selected settings, applies the rendered manifest without writing release values to disk, waits with a fixed timeout, and prints diagnostics if deployment fails.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;h2 id="invoke-and-validate-beam"&gt;Invoke Beam&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Invoke JumpStart endpoints with the released model request schema. For SGLang or vLLM, use the bounded smoke test included in the HyperPod sample.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="invoke-the-deployed-endpoint"&gt;Invoke the deployed endpoint&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Install the sample client dependency.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python3 -m venv .venv
source .venv/bin/activate
python -m pip install -r requirements.txt&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Run a private health check and short request with the recommended starting sampling settings.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python hyperpod_tool.py \
--config config/deployment.env \
smoke sglang \
--prompt "List three checks for a healthy model deployment." \
--temperature 1.0 \
--top-p 0.7&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;
  &lt;p&gt;Replace &lt;code&gt;sglang&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;vllm&lt;/code&gt; for a vLLM deployment. The helper selects a ready worker, opens a temporary port-forward, checks endpoint health, sends the request, and closes the port-forward.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The response resembles the following:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;1. Confirm that the endpoint reports ready.
2. Send a short inference request.
3. Review the service logs for startup errors.&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For an authenticated Inference Gateway, use the bearer-token procedure in the HyperPod sample README. Keep credentials out of source code, manifests, and command history.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="measure-and-tune-performance"&gt;Measure and tune performance&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Test each serving path with repeated, representative coding and tool-use requests at the expected concurrency.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Record the Beam model version, image digest, instance type, precision, context length, parallelism, serving arguments, cache state, and test repetitions.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Measure request success rate, p50 and p95 time to first token, time per output token, end-to-end latency, and aggregate output tokens per second.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Tune batch size, KV cache allocation, replica count, autoscaling, and model and image caching from those results.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Recheck output quality after changing precision or quantization.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Delete resources when you finish testing to avoid ongoing charges.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;For a SageMaker JumpStart real-time endpoint, follow the cleanup instructions in the SageMaker JumpStart deployment sample README.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;For JumpStart, SGLang, or vLLM on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Inference, follow the cleanup instructions in the HyperPod Inference deployment sample README.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Delete a dedicated HyperPod or Amazon EKS cluster created for the walkthrough.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Delete unused cached weights, mirrored artifacts, load balancers, log groups, temporary IAM roles, and captured inference data.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Reflection Beam gives developers an open-weight model designed for agentic coding and multi-step tasks. Start with a SageMaker JumpStart real-time endpoint for a fully managed deployment. Use SageMaker HyperPod Inference with JumpStart when you need Kubernetes-native infrastructure control without managing the serving container, or choose SGLang or vLLM when you need a custom runtime.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Use the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/tree/main/01-models/ReflectionAI/Beam-1.0-501B-A23B" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker JumpStart deployment sample&lt;/a&gt; or the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/tree/main/SageMakerHyperpod/reflection-beam" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HyperPod Inference deployment sample&lt;/a&gt;, then follow the instructions.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To learn more about Reflection AI and its vision for open intelligence, visit &lt;a href="https://reflection.ai/about" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Reflection AI&lt;/a&gt; and read &lt;a href="https://reflection.ai/blog/introducing-beam" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Introducing Beam&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="resources"&gt;Resources&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Reflection Beam checkpoint variants: &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/reflection/Beam-1.0-501B-A23B" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;BF16&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/reflection/Beam-1.0-501B-A23B-FP8" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;FP8&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/reflection/Beam-1.0-501B-A23B-NVFP4" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NVFP4&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/tree/main/01-models/ReflectionAI/Beam-1.0-501B-A23B" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker JumpStart deployment sample&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/tree/main/SageMakerHyperpod/reflection-beam" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HyperPod Inference deployment samples with JumpStart, vLLM, and SGLang&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-model-deployment-deploy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Deploy foundation models from SageMaker JumpStart to HyperPod Inference&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-sagemaker-inference-2026-year-to-date-launches-in-review/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Inference 2026 year-to-date launches in review&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Meta’s Muse Spark Open now available in Amazon SageMaker JumpStart</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/metas-muse-spark-open-now-available-in-amazon-sagemaker-jumpstart/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Aris Tsakpinis]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 15 Oct 2026 17:43:35 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker JumpStart]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">8370f01f72d733de10ec1ce843e1456b54b19515</guid>

					<description>Muse Spark Open, Meta's coding-optimized open-weight model, is now available in Amazon SageMaker JumpStart. Deploy it to a SageMaker AI endpoint in your own AWS account using SageMaker Studio or the SageMaker Python SDK, and run your first inference request.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Muse Spark Open, Meta’s coding-optimized open-weight model, is now available in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt;, the machine learning (ML) hub of &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt;. The model was trained on real developer workflows, such as multi-file refactors and long debugging sessions, and it supports a context window of 1 million tokens. You can deploy it to a SageMaker AI endpoint in your own AWS account in a few steps, without managing the serving infrastructure yourself. SageMaker JumpStart offers two variants: BF16 (&lt;code&gt;Muse-Spark-Open-816B-A42B&lt;/code&gt;) and NVFP4 quantized (&lt;code&gt;Muse-Spark-Open-816B-A42B-NVFP4&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we introduce Muse Spark Open and show you how to deploy the model with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/studio/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Studio&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://sagemaker.readthedocs.io/en/stable/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Python SDK&lt;/a&gt;, send it a first inference request, and clean up.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="overview-of-muse-spark-open"&gt;Overview of Muse Spark Open&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Meta describes Muse Spark Open as a coding-optimized model trained for agentic coding workflows, with a focus on first-attempt accuracy, tool calling, and understanding of large codebases. It’s a reasoning model, and it accepts text, images, documents, audio, and audio-video as input and returns text.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Muse Spark Open uses a mixture-of-experts (MoE) architecture with 816 billion total parameters, of which 42 billion are active for each token. The model holds the capacity of a very large network, while the compute it spends on each token is closer to that of a much smaller dense model. Meta releases the weights under the Muse Model License, which includes the Muse Spark Usage Policy.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following table summarizes the model.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Attribute&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Muse Spark Open&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Provider&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Meta&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Architecture&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Mixture of experts (MoE), reasoning model&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Parameters&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;816B total, 42B active&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Context length&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;1M tokens&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Input modalities&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Text, image, document, audio, audio-video&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Output modalities&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Text&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Variants in SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;BF16 and NVFP4&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;License&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Muse Model License, including the Muse Spark Usage Policy&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;h2 id="built-for-long-running-coding-agents"&gt;Built for long-running coding agents&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Meta trained Muse Spark Open on the way developers work in real repositories: code generation, debugging, multi-file refactors, and tasks that run for a long time. With the 1 million-token context window, the model can take in a whole project, including its dependencies, legacy code, and thousands of files. You don’t need to split a task into smaller pieces to fit the context.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Meta also designed Muse Spark Open as an agentic model from the start. Meta says the model plans, acts, observes the result, and corrects itself over long horizons, rather than making single tool calls. As a reasoning model, it works through a task before it acts, and the SageMaker AI endpoint returns that reasoning alongside the answer, so you can follow what an agent is doing and why. Meta trained the model to browse the web, coordinate subagents for work that can be split up, and operate computers across desktop, mobile, and browser environments. These are capabilities of the model. On SageMaker AI, your application or agent framework provides the tools, browser, and computer-use environment that the model calls. Beyond code, Meta reports that Muse Spark Open reasons across images, documents, audio, and video.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Two properties of the model shape how you serve it. First, sparse activation keeps per-token compute low, but serving still requires enough GPU memory to hold all 816 billion parameters, so the instance choice comes down to memory capacity. Second, the NVFP4 variant stores weights in NVIDIA’s 4-bit floating point format, which needs roughly a quarter of the memory of the BF16 weights. On SageMaker AI, that difference decides the instance: the NVFP4 variant runs on &lt;code&gt;ml.p5e.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, and the BF16 variant runs on &lt;code&gt;ml.p6-b300.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt;. That makes NVFP4 the variant to try first when you size for cost. Evaluate it on your own workloads to confirm the quality fits your use case.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;With open weights on SageMaker AI, you also control the deployment. The endpoint runs in your AWS account, so your source code and prompts are processed on infrastructure that you configure and control. You can attach the endpoint to your own &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/vpc/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC)&lt;/a&gt;, encrypt its storage volume with a key from &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/kms/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS)&lt;/a&gt;, and turn on network isolation for the container. You also choose the instance type and scaling limits, and you pin the model version, so the model only changes when you redeploy.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="use-cases"&gt;Use cases&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;You can use Muse Spark Open for a wide range of applications. Meta’s focus on coding and agentic workflows suits it to the following use cases:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Coding assistants over private repositories&lt;/strong&gt;: Answer questions about large codebases, refactor them, and debug them with the full project in context, while the code is processed on an endpoint in your account.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Long-running coding agents&lt;/strong&gt;: Run agents that plan a change, edit across multiple files, run tests in your own sandbox, and correct course based on the results.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multimodal engineering workflows&lt;/strong&gt;: Turn screenshots, design documents, or a recorded bug report with audio and video into a reproduction plan or a draft fix.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="getting-started-with-sagemaker-jumpstart"&gt;Getting started with SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;You can deploy Muse Spark Open from SageMaker Studio or with the SageMaker Python SDK. Each path creates a real-time SageMaker AI endpoint in your account.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Before you start, make sure that you have the following:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with permissions to create SageMaker AI models and endpoints.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)&lt;/a&gt; execution role for SageMaker AI. For this walkthrough, the &lt;code&gt;AmazonSageMakerFullAccess&lt;/code&gt; managed policy is sufficient. For production, scope the role to the actions and resources that your deployment needs. For details, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-roles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;How to use SageMaker AI execution roles&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;A SageMaker AI domain with access to SageMaker Studio, for the Studio path. For setup instructions, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/onboard-quick-start.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Use quick setup for Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Service quota for the endpoint instance type: &lt;strong&gt;ml.p5e.48xlarge for endpoint usage&lt;/strong&gt; for the NVFP4 variant, or &lt;strong&gt;ml.p6-b300.48xlarge for endpoint usage&lt;/strong&gt; for the BF16 variant. To raise a quota, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/servicequotas/latest/userguide/request-quota-increase.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Requesting a quota increase&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;For the SDK path: Python 3.10 or later and SageMaker Python SDK v3 (&lt;code&gt;pip install --upgrade sagemaker&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS Region where Muse Spark Open is available in SageMaker JumpStart. At launch, Muse Spark Open is available in US East (N. Virginia) and US East (Ohio). To check availability in other Regions, open SageMaker JumpStart in your Region.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The instances that host Muse Spark Open are multi-GPU instances, and you pay for an endpoint for as long as it runs, whether or not it serves requests. Delete the endpoint when you finish, as described in the clean-up section of this post. For rates, refer to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI pricing&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="deploy-using-sagemaker-studio"&gt;Deploy using SageMaker Studio&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The SageMaker Studio path suits a first evaluation: you find the model, review its license, and deploy it from one model detail card.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;To find Muse Spark Open in SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Open SageMaker Studio.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;In the navigation pane, choose &lt;strong&gt;JumpStart&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;In the search box, enter Muse Spark Open.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Muse-Spark-Open-816B-A42B&lt;/strong&gt; for the BF16 variant or &lt;strong&gt;Muse-Spark-Open-816B-A42B-NVFP4&lt;/strong&gt; for the NVFP4 variant to open its model detail card.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Figure 1 shows the model detail card, which lists the model description, license, and the &lt;strong&gt;Deploy&lt;/strong&gt; option.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-22194-1.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-22194-1.jpg" alt="Muse Spark Open model detail card in SageMaker JumpStart with the Deploy option" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The Muse Spark Open model detail card in SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;To deploy the model&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;On the model detail card, choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Muse Spark Open is a gated model, so SageMaker Studio shows its end-user license agreement (EULA). Review the agreement and accept it to continue.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;For &lt;strong&gt;Endpoint name&lt;/strong&gt;, keep the generated name or enter your own.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;For &lt;strong&gt;Instance type&lt;/strong&gt;, select &lt;strong&gt;ml.p5e.48xlarge&lt;/strong&gt; for NVFP4 or &lt;strong&gt;ml.p6-b300.48xlarge&lt;/strong&gt; for BF16. SageMaker Studio lists only the instance types that the model supports.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;For &lt;strong&gt;Initial instance count&lt;/strong&gt;, enter 1.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;(Optional) Under &lt;strong&gt;Advanced options&lt;/strong&gt;, select a VPC and an AWS KMS key, or turn on network isolation.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Figure 2 shows the deployment settings for the NVFP4 variant.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-22194-2.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-22194-2.jpg" alt="Deployment settings for the Muse Spark Open NVFP4 variant in SageMaker JumpStart" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Deployment settings for Muse Spark Open&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Deployment typically takes 15–20 minutes while SageMaker AI provisions the instance and loads the weights. When the endpoint status changes to &lt;strong&gt;InService&lt;/strong&gt;, the endpoint is ready for requests, as shown in Figure 3.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-22194-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/ML-22194-3.png" alt="Muse Spark Open endpoint showing InService status in the SageMaker AI console" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: The Muse Spark Open endpoint in service&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="deploy-using-the-sagemaker-python-sdk"&gt;Deploy using the SageMaker Python SDK&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;SageMaker JumpStart identifies each variant by a model ID:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;BF16: &lt;code&gt;meta-vlm-muse-spark-open-816b-a42b&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;NVFP4: &lt;code&gt;meta-vlm-muse-spark-open-816b-a42b-nvfp4&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following code deploys the NVFP4 variant with SageMaker Python SDK v3. You can run it in a SageMaker Studio notebook, which uses your domain’s execution role. In any other Python environment with AWS credentials, pass your SageMaker AI execution role with the &lt;code&gt;role_arn&lt;/code&gt; parameter. Muse Spark Open is a gated model, so you must set &lt;code&gt;accept_eula=True&lt;/code&gt; to deploy it. To pin a specific model version, add &lt;code&gt;model_version&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;JumpStartConfig&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from sagemaker.core.jumpstart.configs import JumpStartConfig
from sagemaker.serve import ModelBuilder
from sagemaker.train.configs import Compute

# NVFP4 variant; for BF16, use "meta-vlm-muse-spark-open-816b-a42b" with ml.p6-b300.48xlarge
model_id = "meta-vlm-muse-spark-open-816b-a42b-nvfp4"

jumpstart_config = JumpStartConfig(
    model_id=model_id,
    accept_eula=True,  # Confirms that you accept Meta's license terms
)

model_builder = ModelBuilder.from_jumpstart_config(
    jumpstart_config=jumpstart_config,
    compute=Compute(instance_type="ml.p5e.48xlarge"),
    # role_arn="&amp;lt;your-sagemaker-execution-role-arn&amp;gt;",  # Required outside SageMaker Studio
)

model = model_builder.build(model_name="muse-spark-open-nvfp4")
endpoint = model_builder.deploy(endpoint_name="muse-spark-open-nvfp4")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="run-inference"&gt;Run inference&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;When the endpoint is in service, send it a request with the &lt;code&gt;invoke&lt;/code&gt; method. The endpoint accepts an OpenAI-compatible chat payload with a &lt;code&gt;messages&lt;/code&gt; list and returns a chat completion. The final answer is in &lt;code&gt;message.content&lt;/code&gt;, the model’s reasoning is in &lt;code&gt;message.reasoning_content&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;usage.reasoning_tokens&lt;/code&gt; reports how many tokens the reasoning used. Because Muse Spark Open is a reasoning model, leave enough room in &lt;code&gt;max_tokens&lt;/code&gt; for its reasoning and the final answer.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import json

payload = {
    "messages": [
        {
            "role": "user",
            "content": "Write a Python function that validates ISO 8601 dates, and add pytest unit tests for it.",
        }
    ],
    "max_tokens": 4096,
}

response = endpoint.invoke(
    body=json.dumps(payload),
    content_type="application/json",
)

result = json.loads(response.body.read().decode("utf-8"))
message = result["choices"][0]["message"]
print("Reasoning:", message.get("reasoning_content"))
print("Answer:", message["content"])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The endpoint also supports streaming. To receive tokens as they’re generated, add &lt;code&gt;"stream": true&lt;/code&gt; to the request body and call &lt;code&gt;endpoint.invoke_with_response_stream&lt;/code&gt; instead of &lt;code&gt;invoke&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To stop incurring charges, delete the endpoint when you no longer need it. If you deployed with the SDK, delete the model, the endpoint, and the endpoint configuration:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from sagemaker.core.resources import EndpointConfig

endpoint_config = EndpointConfig.get(endpoint_config_name=endpoint.endpoint_name)
model.delete()
endpoint.delete()
endpoint_config.delete()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If you deployed from SageMaker Studio, delete the endpoint in the SageMaker AI console: choose &lt;strong&gt;Inference&lt;/strong&gt;, then &lt;strong&gt;Endpoints&lt;/strong&gt;, select the endpoint, and choose &lt;strong&gt;Actions&lt;/strong&gt;, &lt;strong&gt;Delete&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how to deploy Muse Spark Open, Meta’s coding-optimized open-weight model, from SageMaker JumpStart to an endpoint in your own AWS account. You used SageMaker Studio or the SageMaker Python SDK and sent the model a first inference request. Benchmark both variants on your own repositories before you choose an instance for production.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To get started, search for Muse Spark Open in SageMaker JumpStart in SageMaker Studio. For more deployment options, such as inference components and optimized deployment configurations, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/jumpstart-foundation-models-use.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;JumpStart foundation model usage&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon SageMaker AI Developer Guide. Before you deploy, review the Muse Model License, which SageMaker JumpStart shows when you deploy the model. For help planning a production deployment, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/contact-us/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;contact AWS&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Trust in Depth: Giving agents more autonomy without losing control</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/trust-in-depth-giving-agents-more-autonomy-without-losing-control/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Shawn Bice]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 15 Oct 2026 15:05:35 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Machine Learning]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Artificial Intelligence]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Thought Leadership]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">379390b4bb32581d87fbb3af3ac3000f67e1f7df</guid>

					<description>Enterprise leaders want AI agents to take on more work but don't fully trust them to act correctly every time. Trust in Depth is AWS's layered approach-scope, know, bound, observe, and prove-that lets agents act on their own when their actions can be verified, reserving human judgment for the decisions that need it.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;In every conversation I have with enterprise leaders, they say the same thing. They want agents to do more, but they don’t trust them to do it right all the time.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;That’s fair. The same creativity and autonomy that makes agents useful makes them hard to trust. Give an agent a task and it finds its own path to finish it, picking tools and sequencing steps no developer scripted. Run the same task twice and it might take a different route each time. A test that passes today tells you little about what the agent does tomorrow.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The usual answer is a person in the loop, approving everything the agent does. That works at small scale, but as agents take on more work, human approvals pile up and review fatigue sets in. People asked to check everything stop catching the things that matter. Caution becomes a bottleneck, and agents capable of far more sit idle on the valuable work that could be making more of an impact.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The fix is to let the agent act on its own when its actions can be verified, and reserve human judgment for the decisions that need it. As agents take on higher stakes work, like financial transactions, a wrong action becomes increasingly harmful. Organizations need the confidence to hand an AI agent important work, knowing it will stay within the limits set, even when no one is watching. That confidence must be built.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Content filtering, testing, and monitoring each serve an important purpose, but none of these capabilities alone can promise an agent will behave correctly the next time it runs. Trust comes from independent layers that reinforce one another, the same layered discipline the industry has practiced for two decades as Defense in Depth, turned to a new purpose. Where Defense in Depth limits what can go wrong, this expands what you can safely allow. AWS calls it &lt;strong&gt;Trust in Depth.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="trust-in-depth-trust-you-can-verify"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Trust in Depth: Trust you can verify&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;You build trust by answering a set of questions at different moments in the agent’s lifecycle:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scope, before it runs:&lt;/strong&gt; What is this agent for, what capabilities does the job require, and what must it never do?&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Know, at deployment:&lt;/strong&gt; Who authorized it, what identity does it carry, and can you account for every agent acting on your behalf?&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Bound, while it runs:&lt;/strong&gt; Do its actions stay within the limits you set, and can it still do the job?&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Observe, after each run:&lt;/strong&gt; Can you reconstruct what the agent did and why, and learn from it?&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Across these questions, some requirements can be expressed as precise rules, while others call for judgment. A company might require that only a manager can approve access, or that an employee’s sign-in must be blocked before their mailbox can be shared. Other expectations, such as keeping a friendly tone or handling a sensitive situation appropriately, depend on context and human judgment.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For precisely defined rules, testing shows whether the controls work in the cases you anticipate, but an agent takes paths you didn’t. The technology that goes further is automated reasoning, the mathematical logic that checks whether a rule holds in every possible case. It can establish that a rule holds on every path covered by a precise description of the system and its assumptions, or uncover the exact case where it fails. That result is a proof, and it is only as good as the rules and assumptions behind it, which is why the hardest work comes before the agent runs.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;AWS has &lt;a href="https://www.amazon.science/blog/a-decade-of-mathematical-certainty-reflections-on-the-automated-reasoning-group" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;run automated reasoning in production for more than a decade&lt;/a&gt;, from Identity and Access Management (IAM) at more than 2.1 billion requests per second to critical systems like the AWS Nitro Hypervisor, Amazon S3 Block Public Access, and VPC Reachability Analyzer. Now we are bringing it to agents. Wherever an agent’s rules can be stated precisely, automated reasoning can check them.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;However, not every rule can be stated precisely. “Only a manager can approve access” can be proven. “Keep a friendly tone” cannot. Where proof does not apply, limit the agent’s access and actions, evaluate its behavior, and send genuine ambiguity to a person to apply judgment. Grant more autonomy as you establish that the relevant rules are enforced, the agent performs reliably on the task, and failures can be detected and handled.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Here is what each Trust in Depth layer asks of you, and how AWS helps you answer it.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-141442" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/15/trust-in-depth-hero-image-v2.png" alt="" width="1946" height="1093"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3&gt;Scope your agent to only what the job requires&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Trust begins before an agent runs, and this is where to invest the most effort. Problems caught here are cheap to fix. Scoping means deciding what work the agent owns, which tools, data, and services it may reach, the rules it must follow, and which decisions stay with a person.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;“Resolve the customer’s issue” says what to achieve, not how the agent may act to achieve it. The agent still needs limits on what it may see, what it may do, and when it must stop and ask. Give it only the access its job requires, and no more. Watch for capabilities no one granted on purpose, the ones bundled with a tool or a role. Grant an admin role for one task, and the agent can inherit permissions across your whole environment.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Scoping starts at the build stage, with how you define the agent. &lt;a href="https://kiro.dev/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Kiro&lt;/a&gt;, the agentic coding system, brings rigor and mature engineering practices to the build stage of agents. It turns high-level intent into structured, spec-driven requirements and uses automated reasoning to surface contradictions, ambiguities, and gaps before any code is written. Kiro also derives property-based tests that check the implementation against those requirements across a wide range of inputs, catching bugs that are hard to detect. &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands&lt;/a&gt; provides an open-source tool kit for defining an agent’s model, instructions, and tools, and a managed runtime for operating it. Whatever framework you choose, the responsibility to define the agent’s job and its boundaries is the same.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The next step is turning those boundaries into rules you can hold the agent to. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; provides governance controls to scale agents safely and governs what the agent does. Using Policies in Bedrock AgentCore, you describe in plain language which tools, APIs, and data the agent may reach, and automated reasoning checks the policy for gaps, like catching a rule that grants too much access, and checks for contradictions that make a rule unable to ever be satisfied, before it takes effect.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Scoping your agent is the hard part, because turning human intent into durable, enforceable policy requires precision and completeness. However, it is also the part that pays off most. Everything that builds on what you set here, and the more precise the policy, the more your agent can safely do on its own.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="know-every-agent-acting-on-your-behalf-and-under-whose-authority"&gt;Know every agent acting on your behalf, and under whose authority&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;You can’t govern what you can’t see. Teams are creating agents faster than anyone can track them, and an agent no one knows about is an agent no one controls. Every agent needs its own identity, checked every time it acts. No action should be orphaned. When something goes wrong, you can tell which agent did what, and who authorized it.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/registry.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Agent Registry&lt;/a&gt; provides a managed, approved catalog of the agents, Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers, tools, and skills across your organization. Administrators use approval workflows to gate what becomes discoverable and remove what no longer meets the bar for security, compliance, and quality. Both people and agents can find and reuse what already exists instead of rebuilding it. But a catalog only tells you what exists. Once the agents are known, AgentCore issues each agent its own identity and checks it on every request, so every action traces back to a specific agent operating under a known authority. That is how you know every agent acting on your behalf.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="bound-what-your-agent-says-and-does-within-the-limits-you-set"&gt;Bound what your agent says and does, within the limits you set&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Setting boundaries matters because an agent can take 10 steps you approved and still reach an outcome no one intended. It can expose sensitive data, drift off topic, or combine permitted actions in ways your policies forbid. Limits can fail in both directions. Too loose, and the agent can do what it shouldn’t. Too tight, and it can’t do its job at all. So let the agent choose how to pursue its task but never rely on it to enforce its own limits. Those limits define the agent’s boundaries and belong in the environment around the agent, enforced deterministically and evaluated across the full sequence of actions, not one step at a time.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This is where the policy you defined in Scope takes effect. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Policy in AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; enforces it on every tool call routed through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-transforming-enterprise-ai-agent-tool-development/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;, a secure entry point for agents, tools, and models. Because enforcement sits outside the agent’s reasoning, the same checks can hold even when the agent is buggy, manipulated, or hit with a prompt injection: it cannot turn off a control it does not run. Authority does not have to stay fixed either. Because a task’s risk changes as it proceeds, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/securing-ai-agents-with-temporal-policies-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;temporal policies&lt;/a&gt; weigh what has already happened in a session and adjust permissions as the work continues. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; governs what the agent says and is told. It filters harmful or off-topic content and redacts sensitive data. &lt;a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0uprEe_uCgc" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Automated Reasoning checks&lt;/a&gt; go further, proving a response is consistent with the policy you set, not the model’s interpretation of it.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Boundaries also govern what an agent can reach in the first place: which files it can touch, which services it can call, and which credentials it can use. &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/blog/strands-box-the-big-picture/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Box&lt;/a&gt;, an open-source sandbox, enforces those boundaries from outside the agent’s instructions, so the agent can work with real tools and data but reach only what you allow. The same boundaries hold from a developer’s first local session through to production, so an agent can’t do in production what it couldn’t in development.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="observe-what-your-agent-did-then-improve-it"&gt;Observe what your agent did, then improve it&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Even with scoping, identity, and boundaries in place, an agent can respond in milliseconds, with no errors and every dashboard green, and still do the wrong thing. It may have retrieved the wrong information, chosen the wrong tool, or followed a path no one expected. A healthy system is not the same as a successful task. To tell the difference, you must reconstruct what the agent actually did: the context it received, the tools it called, the policy decisions applied, and the result. Testing before deployment remains essential. Production adds evidence about behavior under real conditions and gives operators what they need to investigate, intervene, and improve.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/omni/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch Omni&lt;/a&gt; is built for continual observation. It captures every trace and lays out each step an agent took, from model calls to tool invocations, on a timeline a team can investigate. Its built-in evaluators score agent responses for qualities standard monitoring misses, such as correctness, faithfulness, and whether the agent chose the right tool. And the traces where an agent went wrong become the dataset you test the fix against, so operating an agent and improving it draw on the same evidence.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="what-trust-in-depth-looks-like-in-practice"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What Trust in Depth looks like in practice&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;A manager asks a help desk agent to offboard Sam, an account manager who is leaving the company. The agent needs to remove Sam’s access, transfer his mailbox to his successor, and free up the license the company no longer needs. But Sam’s emails may also contain evidence needed for a client dispute. The wrong sequence could leave Sam with access after he leaves or put that evidence at risk. Security, messaging, legal, and finance each have requirements the agent must follow, even though no one has scripted the full workflow.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Two rules govern the sequence. Sam’s sign-in must be blocked before his mailbox can be converted and shared with his successor. Before changing his email license, the agent must check for a legal hold, a requirement to preserve records for a dispute, and confirm the change keeps those records protected.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scope&lt;/strong&gt;. Before the agent runs, the team writes these rules as policies in AgentCore. Each policy requires that the prerequisite step already succeeded for Sam’s account before the next step can run. The agent gets exactly six tools: block sign-in, check for legal holds, place a hold, convert a mailbox, give the successor access, and change a license. It gets no tool to delete the account, because the shared mailbox needs the account to survive.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Know&lt;/strong&gt;. The agent carries its own identity, so every change to Sam’s account traces back to it.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Bound&lt;/strong&gt;. The agent tries to convert Sam’s mailbox to a shared one for Priya, the successor. Policy in AgentCore, enforced at the AgentCore Gateway, blocks the call: Sam can still sign in, and converting the mailbox wouldn’t change his password, so his old credentials would still open it. The agent blocks Sam’s sign-in, then converts the mailbox and gives Priya access. Next it moves to the manager’s actual request, freeing up the license, but the gateway blocks that too, because no one has checked Legal’s hold list. The agent checks and finds that Legal requires Sam’s emails to be preserved for a client dispute. It places the hold, confirms it is in effect, then changes Sam’s license to one that supports that protection. The company can reclaim the broader license while keeping the evidence intact.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Observe&lt;/strong&gt;. Every step is recorded: what the agent tried to do, what the rules decided, the hold, and the outcome.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The agent completed the task without a person approving every step. Its permissions limited what it could change, its identity made those changes traceable, and policies stopped it from acting before the required safeguards were in place. The recorded steps let the team verify that Sam’s access was blocked, his successor could use the mailbox, and the required evidence remained protected.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="what-becomes-possible-when-agents-are-trusted"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What becomes possible when agents are trusted&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;AWS brings these layers together so you can scope an agent to its job, know which agents are acting for you, bound what they do and say, see what they did and improve over time, and prove your rules hold wherever they can be stated precisely. No one layer earns that trust on its own. Together, they are Trust in Depth, and they are what turns a capable agent into an agent that can be trusted to do important work.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;You draw the lines: what an agent may do on its own, what needs a person’s approval, and when it must stop. That frees your people for what only people can do: weigh the hard calls, resolve the gray areas, and own the decisions you choose to keep in human hands. As evidence builds that an agent stays inside those lines, you can widen its responsibilities or narrow them.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;At AWS, we believe every organization deploying agents, on any platform, should be able to answer these four questions with evidence, and with proof wherever it applies. That is the bar AWS is building for our customers, and the one we invite the industry to hold itself to. Defense in Depth made it safe to connect systems to the world. Trust in Depth makes it safe to let agents act autonomously within it.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Start with one agent, one workflow, and one boundary you can prove. Then expand as the trust is earned.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To begin implementing enforceable rules, explore &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/guardrails-automated-reasoning-checks.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Automated Reasoning checks in Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;
