  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The compression and zero-issue figures come from Tamnoon’s research. The pattern is consistent, showing fewer open exposures, far less manual work, and no downtime from the fixes themselves.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Building an agent that can reason about a cloud finding is the straightforward part. Building one you’d trust to act on production is the hard part. Amazon Bedrock is a fully managed service that provides access to high-performing foundation models along with the tooling to build agentic applications: orchestration, tool use, and guardrails, through a single API. That combination matters here for a specific reason: acting on production cloud infrastructure demands both strong reasoning and hard safety boundaries.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Four things make that possible:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Your exposures actually close: Tamnoon operates on live cloud infrastructure, not on tickets or workflow automation. The backlog goes down instead of sideways.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Autonomy scales without production risk: Every proposed action carries a confidence rating. SAFE actions can auto-execute while AWAITING DATA or RISKY actions pull in Tamnoon’s embedded cloud engineers.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Runs on the stack you already bought: Tamnoon works on top of popular CNAPPs and cloud security tooling. Every detection tool you already pay for starts paying off because its findings finally get closed.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Team capacity scales without growing: Fast, safe adoption of frontier AI means more exposures closed per analyst, with no added headcount.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="next-steps"&gt;Next steps&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To get started with a similar architecture, consider the following:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Explore &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; and the models available through it.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Review the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock documentation&lt;/a&gt; to plan your architecture.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://tamnoon.io/demo/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Book a demo&lt;/a&gt; with Tamnoon to find out how much of your critical cloud exposure you can close in the next 90 days.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Deploy and evaluate models with Amazon SageMaker JumpStart</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/deploy-and-evaluate-models-with-amazon-sagemaker-jumpstart/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Vamsi Goparaju]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 14 Oct 2026 13:14:36 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker JumpStart]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">e6ff6df24e30f88e005340fdee64687eef73ffa9</guid>

					<description>Learn how to deploy an available model from Amazon SageMaker JumpStart, run a lightweight and repeatable performance test using the SageMaker Python SDK, and delete the endpoint when testing is complete.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Choosing the right model for a specific application requires a practical way to test it with representative inputs. Amazon SageMaker JumpStart provides access to a broad catalog of already pretrained models. In this post, we show how to deploy an available model from SageMaker JumpStart, run a lightweight and repeatable performance test using the SageMaker Python SDK, and delete the endpoint when testing is complete. For supported models and available capacity, this workflow provides a repeatable evaluation path, although deployment time varies by model, instance type, quota, and AWS Region.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In 2026, SageMaker JumpStart introduced two improvements that simplify model discovery and deployment. In the Model Hub section of the Amazon SageMaker AI console, you can browse the SageMaker JumpStart catalog, view sample notebooks, and open them directly in SageMaker Studio. Pre-configured endpoint deployment options provide named configurations optimized for priorities such as low cost, low latency, or general-purpose workloads across a range of generative AI models, including Neuron-optimized configurations. Additionally, you can now go from Hugging Face model discovery to SageMaker AI deployment in a single click using the new deep link integration.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Our approach uses a reusable Python script that performs three steps:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Discover and deploy a model from SageMaker JumpStart.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Run model-specific sample prompts and measure latency, approximate output length, and single-request generation rate.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Clean up the endpoint.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This pattern works for many compatible SageMaker JumpStart models, although payload formats, required instance types, end-user license agreement (EULA) requirements, and response schemas may require model-specific adjustments.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with SageMaker AI permissions.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3+ with SageMaker Python SDK v3 installed (&lt;code&gt;pip install "sagemaker&amp;gt;=3,&amp;lt;4"&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Sufficient GPU instance quota for the model you want to test or &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/studio-updated-launch.html#studio-updated-launch-quotas" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;request a quota increase&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="step-1.a-discover-available-models"&gt;Step 1.a: Discover available models&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;UI path to find models in SageMaker JumpStart:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22153-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22153-1.png" alt="SageMaker Studio JumpStart Models page showing the model catalog with spotlight and text generation models and filters" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Browsing the model catalog on the SageMaker JumpStart Models page in SageMaker Studio&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Code path using SageMaker Python SDK:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from sagemaker.core.jumpstart.notebook_utils import (
    list_jumpstart_models,
)

REGION = "us-east-1"

models = list_jumpstart_models(
    filter="task == textgeneration",
    region=REGION,
    list_versions=True,
)

print(f"Found {len(models)} text generation models")

for model_id, model_version in models[:10]:
    print(
        f" - {model_id} "
        f"(version {model_version})"
    )&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Or search by model name:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from sagemaker.core.jumpstart.notebook_utils import list_jumpstart_models

ids = list_jumpstart_models(
    filter="model_id contains qwen",
    region=REGION,
    list_versions=True,
)

print(f"{len(ids)} models matched in {REGION}\n")

for model_id, version in ids:
    print(f"{model_id:&amp;lt;55} v{version}")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="step-1.b-deploy-the-model"&gt;Step 1.b: Deploy the model&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;UI path to deploy models from SageMaker JumpStart:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22153-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22153-2.png" alt="JumpStart model detail page for Qwen3.8 27B with the Deploy button highlighted and model capabilities listed" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Deploying a model from its SageMaker JumpStart detail page in SageMaker Studio&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Code path using SageMaker Python SDK:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import time

from sagemaker.core.jumpstart.accessors import JumpStartModelsAccessor
from sagemaker.core.jumpstart.configs import JumpStartConfig
from sagemaker.core.training.configs import Compute
from sagemaker.serve import ModelBuilder

MODEL_ID = "huggingface-vlm-qwen3-5-27b"
MODEL_VERSION = "1.13.0"
REGION = "us-east-1"
ACCEPT_EULA = False  # Set True only after reviewing the model terms

def get_field(value, name, default=None):
    if isinstance(value, dict):
        return value.get(name, default)
    return getattr(value, name, default)

# Resolve the JumpStart-recommended deployment configuration.
specs = JumpStartModelsAccessor().get_model_specs(
    region=REGION,
    model_id=MODEL_ID,
    version=MODEL_VERSION,
)

if isinstance(specs, list):
    if not specs:
        raise ValueError(
            f"No JumpStart specification found for {MODEL_ID}"
        )
    specs = specs[0]

recommended_instance_type = get_field(
    specs,
    "default_inference_instance_type",
)

if not recommended_instance_type:
    raise ValueError(
        f"No recommended instance type found for {MODEL_ID}"
    )

print(f"Model: {MODEL_ID}")
print(f"Region: {REGION}")
print(f"Recommended instance: {recommended_instance_type}")

start = time.time()

jumpstart_config = JumpStartConfig(
    model_id=MODEL_ID,
    model_version=MODEL_VERSION,
    accept_eula=ACCEPT_EULA,
)

model_builder = ModelBuilder.from_jumpstart_config(
    jumpstart_config=jumpstart_config,
    compute=Compute(
        instance_type=recommended_instance_type,
        instance_count=1,
    ),
)

model = model_builder.build()
endpoint = model_builder.deploy()
deploy_time = time.time() - start

print(f"Deployed in {deploy_time:.1f} seconds")
print(f"Endpoint: {endpoint.endpoint_name}")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="step-2-run-sample-prompts-and-measure-performance"&gt;Step 2: Run sample prompts and measure performance&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from sagemaker.core.payloads import retrieve_all_examples
import json
import time

MODEL_ID = "huggingface-vlm-qwen3-5-27b"
MODEL_VERSION = "1.13.0"
REGION = "us-east-1"

example_payloads = retrieve_all_examples(
    region=REGION,
    model_id=MODEL_ID,
    model_version=MODEL_VERSION,
    serialize=True,
)

#you can re-create the endpoint pointer using the endpoint name:
#from sagemaker.core.resources import Endpoint
#endpoint = Endpoint.get(endpoint_name="&amp;lt;your_endpoint_name&amp;gt;",region=REGION)

def read_response_body(result):
    body = result.body
    if hasattr(body, "read"):
        body = body.read()
    if isinstance(body, bytes):
        body = body.decode("utf-8")
    if isinstance(body, str):
        try:
            return json.loads(body)
        except json.JSONDecodeError:
            return body
    return body

def extract_output(response):
    """Handle common JumpStart text-generation response formats."""
    if isinstance(response, list):
        response = response[0] if response else ""
    if isinstance(response, dict):
        choices = response.get("choices")
        if choices:
            choice = choices[0]
            message = choice.get("message")
            if isinstance(message, dict):
                return message.get("content", "")
            return choice.get("text", "")
        for field in (
            "generated_text",
            "output_text",
            "text",
        ):
            if field in response:
                return response[field]
    return response

results = []

for payload in example_payloads or []:
    start = time.perf_counter()
    result = endpoint.invoke(
        body=payload.body,
        content_type=payload.content_type,
        accept=payload.accept,
    )
    latency = time.perf_counter() - start
    response = read_response_body(result)
    output = extract_output(response)
    if not isinstance(output, str):
        output = json.dumps(output, indent=2)
    # This is an estimate based on whitespace-separated words.
    output_words = len(output.split())
    results.append(
        {
            "request_preview": payload.body[:50],
            "latency_s": round(latency, 2),
            "estimated_output_words": output_words,
            "words_per_second": round(
                output_words / latency,
                1,
            ) if latency &amp;gt; 0 else 0,
        }
    )
    print("Input:\n", payload.body, end="\n\n")
    print("Output:\n", output, end="\n\n")
    print(
        f"Latency: {latency:.2f} seconds\n"
        f"Estimated output words: {output_words}\n"
        f"Estimated single-request output generation rate: "
        f"{output_words / latency:.1f} words/second\n"
    )
    print("-" * 80)

print("\nSummary:")
for result in results:
    print(result)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Result:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;Input:
{"max_tokens": 256, "messages": [{"content": "What is deep learning?", "role": "user"}], "model": "/opt/ml/model"}
Output:
Here's a thinking process that leads to the explanation of deep learning:
1. **Deconstruct the Request:**
* **Question:** "What is deep learning?"
* **Intent:** The user wants a clear, comprehensive, yet accessible definition and overview of deep learning.
* **Target Audience:** Likely someone looking for a general understanding, possibly a student, tech
enthusiast, or professional exploring the field. The tone should be informative, structured, and
not overly jargon-heavy (though key terms need explanation).
2. **Initial Brainstorming &amp;amp; Key Concepts:**
* What is it? A subset of machine learning, which is a subset of AI.
* Core mechanism: Artificial Neural Networks (ANNs).
* "Deep" means many layers (hidden layers).
* How does it work? Learning representations from data (feature learning).
* Key applications: Image recognition, NLP, speech, self-driving cars.
* Why is it popular now? Big data, GPU power, better algorithms.
* Pros/Cons: High accuracy vs. black box, data hunger.
3. **Structuring the Answer:**
Latency: 9.07 seconds
Estimated output words: 153
Estimated single-request output generation rate: 16.9 words/second
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Summary:
{'request_preview': '{"max_tokens": 256, "messages": {"content": "What', 'latency_s': 9.07, 'estimated_output_words': 153, 'words_per_second': 16.9}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="step-3-cleanup"&gt;Step 3: Cleanup&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;print(f"Deleting endpoint: {endpoint.endpoint_name}")
endpoint.delete()
endpoint.wait_for_delete()
print("Endpoint deleted")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This pattern works for many available SageMaker JumpStart models, but the payload format, required instance type, EULA, and response parsing may require model-specific adjustments. By using consistent test prompts and measuring latency, throughput, and output quality across models, you can compare options and select the right fit for your workload.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Try this approach with different models from Amazon SageMaker JumpStart to find the right balance of quality, speed, and cost for your applications.&lt;/p&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Building agents that transact with Anchor Browser and AgentCore payments</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/building-agents-that-transact-with-anchor-browser-and-agentcore-payments/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Cher Simon]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 13 Oct 2026 20:10:33 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">0c79d3a743bd70e676fa029365ec191931b2b410</guid>

					<description>Learn how the AWS Generative AI Innovation Center Partner Agent Factory and Anchor Browser built AI agents that autonomously pay for x402-protected web resources and APIs. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments checks budgets, signs transactions, and retries requests without exposing wallet credentials to the agent runtime.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;AI agents increasingly need to access web resources that are priced per request. For example, premium datasets charge by the query, research APIs meter by the call, and paywalled publishers gate content behind payment. When an AI agent encounters one of these resources, the server returns HTTP 402 Payment Required. The agent has no built-in payment mechanism, no budget attached to the session, and no programmatic way to satisfy the request. The task stalls and requires manual intervention.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/marketplace/pp/prodview-ujbrqwp274l6g" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Anchor Browser with Agentic Payments&lt;/a&gt; handles machine-to-machine payment workflows on behalf of the agent. Developed in collaboration with the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/awsmarketplace/aws-genaiic-partner-agent-factory-new-ai-agents-now-in-aws-marketplace/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Generative AI Innovation Center Partner Agent Factory&lt;/a&gt;, it’s now available on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/marketplace/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Marketplace&lt;/a&gt;. This post covers how &lt;a href="https://anchorbrowser.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Anchor Browser&lt;/a&gt; integrates with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments&lt;/a&gt; so that AI agents can pay for x402-protected pages and APIs autonomously. The x402 protocol is an open standard for HTTP-based machine-to-machine payments. A server responds with 402 Payment Required along with the amount, accepted payment methods, and payment address that the agent uses to complete the transaction.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Anchor Browser is a cloud-based browser automation solution purpose-built for AI agents. Each agent session runs inside a managed browser environment that maintains authentication state, handles navigation, captures network requests, and provides a live view of page interactions.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore provides capabilities to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments is a fully managed capability within AgentCore that AI agents can use to discover, access, and pay for APIs, Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers, and web content autonomously. AgentCore payments manages wallet connectivity, enforces configurable spending limits at the infrastructure layer, and processes payments through the x402 protocol. It also provides observability into payment attempts, approvals, denials, and settlements through Amazon CloudWatch.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;When an agent running in Anchor Browser encounters a 402 response, AgentCore payments authorizes and completes the transaction within the configured budget. The workflow continues without interruption.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="challenges"&gt;Challenges&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;When an autonomous agent encounters an x402-protected resource, the workflow stalls. The agent makes a request, such as fetching a premium research report or calling a paid data enrichment API, and the server responds with HTTP 402 Payment Required. Without a mechanism to pay, the task that was supposed to run autonomously now requires human intervention.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Today, developers must build the entire payment stack. This effort spans four categories of complexity:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Payment protocol handling&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Detecting and parsing the 402 challenge from the HTTP response, and distinguishing payment-required responses from other 4xx errors.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Constructing the correct payment header with the cryptographic proof in the exact format the merchant expects.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Supporting multiple &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments-process-payment.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;payment schemes&lt;/a&gt; (exact, upto, and protocol variants) without hardcoding vendor-specific logic into the agent.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Maintaining compatibility across x402 merchant implementations that might vary in challenge format, supported stablecoins, or settlement timing.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Security and key management&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Storing a private key to sign payments, requiring secure key storage within an environment exposed to untrusted inputs.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Managing wallet lifecycle operations, including key rotation, credential refresh, and provider failover.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Operational resilience&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Resubmitting the original request with the payment header while preserving session state, cookies, and request context.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Providing idempotency, which is essential for wallet operations.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Enforcing per-session and per-transaction spend limits with real-time budget tracking across concurrent agent executions.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Observability and compliance&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Capturing every payment attempt, approval, denial, and settlement in audit logs for compliance and reconciliation.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Without a managed payment service, the agent must internalize the entire payment stack. On every x402-protected call that returns a 402 response, the agent must orchestrate the full payment flow internally, including:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Signing the proof.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Attaching the correct header.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Retrying the request.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Settling the transaction.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Logging the result.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Each of these steps requires custom code that developers must build, secure, and maintain. Figure 1 illustrates this architecture. The agent contains eight tightly coupled components (Wallet, Private Keys, Budget Controls, Sign Proof, Attach Header, Retry Logic, Settle Payment, and Logging). The agent connects to an x402 API with a 402 challenge flowing back and a retry flowing forward.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-21771-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-21771-1-1.png" alt="Agent payment stack with eight embedded components connecting to an x402 API, showing a 402 challenge returning and a retry sent forward" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Agent payment stack&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The security considerations extend beyond the engineering effort.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Uncontrolled spending.&lt;/strong&gt; Without hard spending limits enforced at the infrastructure level, a bug, retry loop, prompt injection, or inadvertent access can trigger unintended payments. An agent retrying a paid endpoint in a loop continues to authorize payments until manual intervention occurs.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Key exposure.&lt;/strong&gt; When private keys reside inside the agent runtime, they share an execution environment with untrusted web pages, third-party tools, and external dependencies. These vectors might inadvertently disclose the credentials that control real funds.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Signing flow exposure.&lt;/strong&gt; If the signing infrastructure is accessible from the runtime, a threat actor can attempt to access the key or redirect payments to an address under unauthorized control. Effective mitigation requires that the key doesn’t enter the runtime in the first place.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Browser-based agents introduce an additional constraint: the payment must occur within the same browser session. Otherwise, session recordings and live view no longer match what actually executed.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="solution"&gt;Solution&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Anchor Browser and AgentCore payments address this complexity by moving the entire payment stack into managed infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Unlike a stateless tool call, Anchor Browser provides a real cloud browser with persistent identity, repeatable and deterministic workflows, and the session context needed to log in, navigate, and finish tasks reliably. Authentication, navigation, network requests, recordings, and live view happen inside the same browser session. When a payment is required, the transaction happens within that session. The recording captures the full sequence from request to 402 to payment to content delivery. Anchor agents include a built-in x402 tool that handles paid API calls natively within the browser context. The built-in tool requires no changes to existing Playwright, Puppeteer, or Chrome DevTools Protocol-based workflows.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore payments serves as the payment layer. At the center of the service is the Payment Manager, the top-level resource that coordinates payment operations for the AWS account. Creating a Payment Manager requires specifying an authorizer type and an IAM role. AgentCore then provisions a corresponding workload identity in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The remaining components connect the Payment Manager to external wallet infrastructure:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;A &lt;strong&gt;PaymentConnector&lt;/strong&gt; integrates the Payment Manager with an external wallet provider, either Coinbase Developer Platform (Coinbase CDP) or Stripe Privy.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;A &lt;strong&gt;PaymentCredentialProvider&lt;/strong&gt; stores wallet provider credentials securely in AgentCore Identity.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;A &lt;strong&gt;PaymentInstrument&lt;/strong&gt; identifies the specific wallet address from which funds are drawn.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Anchor Browser sets up wallets through AgentCore payments using a supported wallet provider, without placing wallet secrets or private keys inside the agent runtime. AgentCore payments handles wallet operations through the configured provider. For endpoint discovery, AgentCore provides a curated &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments-connect-bazaar.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Coinbase x402 Bazaar MCP server through Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;, so agents can discover available pay-per-use x402 endpoints programmatically.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore payments restructures the payment architecture. Sensitive operations move out of the agent runtime and into a managed service layer. The Anchor Agent holds only two payment-related components: a budget session and the x402 tool. The infrastructure, including authentication, wallet secrets, payment limits, observability, and payment proof signing, resides in the AgentCore payments layer. Figure 2 shows the Anchor Agent connecting to the AgentCore payments managed layer, which handles wallet provider integration with Coinbase CDP or Stripe Privy.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-21771-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-21771-2-1.png" alt="Anchor Agent connecting to the AgentCore payments managed layer, which integrates with Coinbase CDP or Stripe Privy wallet providers" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Anchor Browser integrates with AgentCore payments&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h3 id="how-the-payment-flow-works"&gt;How the payment flow works&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following steps describe what happens end to end, from the moment a browser session encounters an x402-protected resource to the moment the agent receives the response and continues the task.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Anchor Browser creates a dedicated AgentCore PaymentSession for the browser session.&lt;/strong&gt; When the session starts with x402 turned on, Anchor Browser provisions a PaymentSession through AgentCore payments, linking the browser session to a configured payment instrument and budget.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The browser receives an HTTP 402 Payment Required response.&lt;/strong&gt; The agent navigates to a URL or makes a fetch or XMLHttpRequest (XHR) request. The merchant responds with a 402 challenge specifying the amount, accepted payment methods, and payment address.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The browser selects a compatible payment method.&lt;/strong&gt; Anchor Browser reads the 402 challenge payload and matches the requested payment method against the instrument linked to the PaymentSession.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AgentCore verifies the session budget and generates the cryptographic payment proof.&lt;/strong&gt; AgentCore payments validates that the payment amount falls within the session spending limit. The service then signs the transaction using the configured wallet provider (Coinbase CDP or Stripe Privy) and returns the cryptographic proof. No key material enters the browser or agent runtime.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The browser retries the original request with the payment header.&lt;/strong&gt; The browser attaches the signed proof as &lt;code&gt;PAYMENT-SIGNATURE&lt;/code&gt; for x402 v2 or &lt;code&gt;X-PAYMENT&lt;/code&gt; for x402 v1, then retries the original request exactly once.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The merchant verifies and settles the payment, then returns the protected resource.&lt;/strong&gt; The merchant validates the cryptographic proof, settles the transaction on-chain, and serves the content. The agent continues the task.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Wallet credentials remain with AgentCore and the configured wallet provider throughout this flow.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Before you begin, make sure that you have a valid Anchor Browser API key (&lt;code&gt;ANCHOR_API_KEY&lt;/code&gt;). To create one, see the Anchor Browser &lt;a href="https://docs.anchorbrowser.io/quickstart/use-via-api" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Quick Start&lt;/a&gt; guide.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="setting-up-automatic-x402-payments"&gt;Setting up automatic x402 payments&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Setting up agentic payments for Anchor Browser requires one session property. Set &lt;code&gt;session.x402&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;true&lt;/code&gt;. The property defaults to &lt;code&gt;false&lt;/code&gt;. When enabled, the browser session can automatically access x402-protected websites with payments signed by AgentCore payments and retried inside the same browser session. Enabling &lt;code&gt;session.x402&lt;/code&gt; authorizes the browser to make real payments from the configured payment instrument.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following examples show how to create a payment-enabled session for Anchor Browser using cURL and JavaScript:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;cURL&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;curl --request POST
--url https://api.anchorbrowser.io/v1/sessions
--header "anchor-api-key: $ANCHOR_API_KEY"
--header "content-type: application/json"
--data '{"session":{"x402":true}}'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;JavaScript&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-javascript"&gt;const response = await fetch('https://api.anchorbrowser.io/v1/sessions', {
  method: 'POST',
  headers: {
    'anchor-api-key': process.env.ANCHOR_API_KEY,
    'content-type': 'application/json',
  },
  body: JSON.stringify({ session: { x402: true } }),
});
const { data: session } = await response.json();
console.log(session.cdp_url);&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After connected through Chrome DevTools Protocol, the agent navigates normally. You don’t need to include a wallet key or payment header in your automation code. The browser handles 402 challenges, payment signing, and retries transparently for document navigation, fetch, and XHR requests.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="payment-session-limits-and-behavior"&gt;Payment session limits and behavior&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Anchor Browser enforces the following guardrails at the session level through AgentCore payments integration. Each payment-ready browser session operates within configured spending and time constraints. For specific limits, refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.anchorbrowser.io/advanced/automatic-x402-payments" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Anchor Browser technical documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If the merchant response is malformed, no compatible network is offered, the budget is exhausted, or AgentCore payments rejects the payment, the browser returns the original 402 response to the page unchanged. Your automation code can then handle the 402 as a standard HTTP error.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The x402 session property controls payments to third-party resources only. The property doesn’t replace Anchor credits and doesn’t pay for the browser session.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="automation-code-for-anchor-browser"&gt;Automation code for Anchor Browser&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Your automation code for Anchor Browser remains unchanged. The following example demonstrates that no payment-specific code is required:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-javascript"&gt;const { chromium } = require('playwright');
// Connect to the x402-enabled session via CDP
const browser = await chromium.connectOverCDP(session.cdp_url);
const page = await browser.newPage();
// Navigate to an x402-protected resource exactly as with a free page
await page.goto('https://premium-research.example.com/market-report');
// The page loaded, the payment was handled automatically
// Extract content as normal
const content = await page.textContent('article');&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;No wallet logic, no payment header construction, and no retry code. The agent navigates to the URL. If the server returns 402, Anchor Browser coordinates with AgentCore payments to sign the payment proof, retries the request with the appropriate payment header, and delivers the content to the page. Your automation code receives the final rendered page with no indication that a payment occurred.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This pattern applies uniformly to paywalled journalism, licensed financial data, gated API documentation, metered research databases, and other resources that gate content behind a 402 challenge.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="using-the-built-in-x402-tool-for-api-calls"&gt;Using the built-in x402 tool for API calls&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Paid resources aren’t limited to web pages. Many are APIs that expect POST, PUT, or other HTTP methods. Common examples include data enrichment services, premium inference endpoints, and licensed content APIs. For these use cases, Anchor Browser agents include a built-in x402 tool that you call like a standard HTTPS API within the browser context.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Your agent makes the API call through fetch or XHR. If the server returns a 402 challenge, the browser coordinates payment signing through AgentCore payments and retries the request with the signed payment header. Your code receives the response as though the endpoint were free. The tool isn’t tied to a specific vendor, model, or framework. Payment operates as a session-level capability beneath your existing tooling, and no changes to agent code are required.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-javascript"&gt;// Fetch call to a paid API endpoint within the x402-enabled session
const response = await page.evaluate(async () =&amp;gt; {
  const res = await fetch('https://data-enrichment.example.com/api/v1/enrich', {
    method: 'POST',
    headers: { 'content-type': 'application/json' },
    body: JSON.stringify({ company_domain: 'example.com' }),
  });
  return res.json();
});
// If the API returned 402, AgentCore handled payment automatically
// The response contains the enriched data
console.log(response);&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Your automation code doesn’t receive wallet credentials. Automatic payments are available only when &lt;code&gt;session.x402&lt;/code&gt; is set to &lt;code&gt;true&lt;/code&gt; and remain subject to the spending and expiration limits described in the previous section.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="key-benefits"&gt;Key benefits&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This integration moves payment from application code to the infrastructure layer:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No payment infrastructure to build.&lt;/strong&gt; No wallet SDK to integrate, no 402 response parser to write, no retry loop to implement, no header construction logic to maintain, and no custom audit logging to build. AgentCore payments manages the entire payment stack.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No changes to existing tooling.&lt;/strong&gt; Playwright scripts, Puppeteer automations, Chrome DevTools Protocol commands, and agent flows in Anchor Browser continue to function exactly as before. Payment is a session capability attached to the browser, not a modification to agent logic, prompts, or tool definitions.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Private keys don’t enter the runtime.&lt;/strong&gt; Signing and settlement happen inside AgentCore payments. The service communicates with Coinbase CDP or Stripe Privy through secure credential storage in AgentCore Identity. The agent runtime doesn’t have access to key material that can move funds.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Budgets are enforced at the infrastructure level.&lt;/strong&gt; Every session operates within defined spend limits that AgentCore enforces before authorizing a payment. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Observability provides full payment audit trails through Amazon CloudWatch. When a session reaches the spending limit, AgentCore payments denies the payment.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Paid tasks complete successfully.&lt;/strong&gt; Paid GET pages load. Paid API calls return responses. The agent completes the workflow rather than stopping at a 402 response and requiring manual intervention.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Billing is consolidated.&lt;/strong&gt; Anchor Browser settles third-party x402 charges in credits on the same invoice as browser session time. There’s no separate crypto custody to manage, no USDC balance to monitor, and no on-chain settlement for the finance team to reconcile.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Anchor Browser, integrated with AgentCore payments, equips AI agents to handle x402 payment challenges autonomously for paywalled web pages and paid API endpoints. Wallet credentials stay out of agent code, and no custom payment infrastructure is required.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;As more of the web moves to per-request pricing, agents that lack a payment mechanism can’t complete tasks involving paid resources. This integration addresses that gap: agents complete paid workflows end to end, operating within defined budgets, with full observability through AgentCore Observability, and without cryptographic key material in the runtime.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To get started, explore the following resources:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.anchorbrowser.io/advanced/automatic-x402-payments" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Automatic x402 payments in Anchor Browser&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore payments developer guide&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments-browser.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore payments with browser integration&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/marketplace/pp/prodview-ujbrqwp274l6g?sr=0-2&amp;amp;ref_=beagle&amp;amp;applicationId=AWSMPContessa" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Anchor Browser on AWS Marketplace&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;

 &lt;/div&gt;
 &lt;div class="blog-author-box" style="padding-top: 2.0em"&gt;
  &lt;div class="blog-author-image" style="margin-right: 1.0em"&gt;
   &lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignnone size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-21771-6-1.jpg" alt="Chethan Shriyan" width="100" height="133"&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;
  &lt;h3 class="lb-h4"&gt;Chethan Shriyan&lt;/h3&gt;
  &lt;p style="overflow: hidden"&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/chethanshriyan/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Chethan&lt;/a&gt; is a Principal Product Manager – Technical at AWS. He has more than 12 years of experience in product and business management. Chethan is passionate about building and delivering technology products that create meaningful impact in customers’ lives.&lt;/p&gt;
 &lt;/div&gt;
 &lt;div class="blog-author-box" style="padding-top: 2.0em"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Manage private pricing offers for Amazon Bedrock with managed entitlements</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/manage-private-pricing-offers-for-amazon-bedrock-with-managed-entitlements/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Daniel Wirjo]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 13 Oct 2026 17:17:25 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">96270fda2506ac4609e9172e331312d358ae21d1</guid>

					<description>Managing AI model access across dozens or hundreds of AWS accounts creates friction when each account must accept AWS Marketplace Private Pricing Offers individually. This post shows two ways to activate MPPO entitlements in AWS License Manager: a lightweight script for a one-time fix, and CDK automation for ongoing event-driven processing.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Managing AI model access across dozens or hundreds of AWS accounts creates operational friction when each workload account must subscribe and accept AWS Marketplace Private Pricing Offers (MPPOs) individually. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/simplify-multi-account-access-to-amazon-bedrock-models-with-managed-entitlements/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock with managed entitlements&lt;/a&gt; simplifies multi-account access to third-party AI models so you can subscribe once from a central account and distribute access across your organization.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we show you two ways to activate AWS Marketplace Private Pricing Offer (MPPO) entitlements in AWS License Manager. Use the lightweight script when you want a one-time, no-infrastructure fix for received licenses you already trust. Use the CDK automation when you want ongoing event-driven processing that verifies sellers, creates and activates distributed grants.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="why-automate-grant-activation"&gt;Why automate grant activation&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;With AWS Marketplace Private Pricing Offers, you can negotiate custom rates for third-party models on Amazon Bedrock. After an eligible private offer is accepted, AWS Marketplace creates a license in AWS License Manager. In AWS Organizations with all features enabled, organization-level grants are auto-accepted but remain Disabled until you activate them.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Teams often manage this by checking the AWS License Manager console and activating grants manually. Manual checks work, but they add effort and are easy to miss. Until someone activates the grant, negotiated pricing does not apply. The same effort appears as teams adopt new models or renew terms.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The sample repository provides two paths:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Path 1 – The lightweight path&lt;/strong&gt; is a standalone script that lists received licenses and prints a dry-run plan. It only creates and activates organization-wide grants when you re-run it with &lt;code&gt;--apply --confirm-account-id&lt;/code&gt;. It has no seller allow-list, so use it only when you trust every received license in the management account.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Path 2 – The event-driven path&lt;/strong&gt; uses AWS Cloud Development Kit (AWS CDK) to deploy ongoing automation in your AWS management account.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Choose one of the following implementation paths. Use Path 1 for one-time activation without CDK. Use Path 2 for ongoing event-driven automation.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="path-1-lightweight-script-one-time-activation"&gt;Path 1: Lightweight script (one-time activation)&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Use this path when you need one-time activation without deploying CDK. The script lists every received license in the management account, so use the dry run as your review gate before applying changes.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Clone the sample repository locally and move into the project directory.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;git clone https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-managed-entitlements.git
cd sample-bedrock-managed-entitlements&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Run the lightweight script in dry-run mode.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python3 lightweight/distribute_licenses.py&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Review every listed license and confirm that the ISV Partner issuer and account scope match the private offer you expect.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Apply the reviewed plan from the same AWS Organizations management account.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python3 lightweight/distribute_licenses.py --apply --confirm-account-id ACCOUNT_ID&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;After the grant is active, validate the billing path before you rely on the private offer in production. Billing data in AWS Cost Explorer can lag, so use it to compare the effective charge for covered Amazon Bedrock usage after charges appear. For a repeatable check, adapt the sample &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-managed-entitlements/blob/main/scripts/bedrock_discount_check.py"&gt;scripts/bedrock_discount_check.py&lt;/a&gt; utility to match your ISV Partner issuer and confirm the expected discount path.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;h3 id="path-2-cdk-automation-ongoing-activation"&gt;Path 2: CDK automation (ongoing activation)&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Use this path when you want new private pricing agreements handled automatically. The CDK stack listens for eligible AWS Marketplace agreement events, checks your seller allowlist, creates distributed grants, and retries activation when License Manager needs more time. The solution contains three components:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon EventBridge rule&lt;/strong&gt;: The stack listens for AWS Marketplace agreement events on the default event bus in &lt;code&gt;us-east-1&lt;/code&gt;. The rule matches source &lt;code&gt;aws.agreement-marketplace&lt;/code&gt; and detail type &lt;code&gt;Purchase Agreement Created - Acceptor&lt;/code&gt;, then invokes the AWS Lambda function.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Lambda function&lt;/strong&gt;: The function receives the AWS Marketplace agreement event, verifies the seller allow-list, discovers the license, creates the distributed grant, and requests activation with &lt;code&gt;CreateGrantVersion(Status=ACTIVE)&lt;/code&gt;. The function logs the outcome to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonCloudWatch/latest/logs/WhatIsCloudWatchLogs.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch Logs&lt;/a&gt; for auditability.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS License Manager entitlements&lt;/strong&gt;: The grant targets your organization, selected organizational units (OUs), or specific accounts. Negotiated pricing applies only after the grant is activated and reaches &lt;code&gt;ACTIVE&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;WORKFLOW_COMPLETED&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the event-driven flow.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div id="attachment_141026" style="width: 1490px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/21638-1.png"&gt;&lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-141026" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-141026" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/21638-1.png" alt="Sequence diagram showing AWS Marketplace sending a Purchase Agreement Created - Acceptor event to Amazon EventBridge, which invokes an AWS Lambda function. The function verifies the seller, discovers the received license, creates a distributed grant, requests activation with CreateGrantVersion(Status=ACTIVE), and writes the outcome to Amazon CloudWatch Logs." width="1480" height="721"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-141026" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Sequence diagram showing AWS Marketplace sending a Purchase Agreement Created – Acceptor event to Amazon EventBridge, which invokes an AWS Lambda function. The function verifies the seller, discovers the received license, creates a distributed grant, requests activation with CreateGrantVersion(Status=ACTIVE), and writes the outcome to Amazon CloudWatch Logs.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Key benefits of this approach:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Faster time to negotiated pricing.&lt;/strong&gt; The negotiated rate applies after the distributed grant reaches &lt;code&gt;ACTIVE&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;WORKFLOW_COMPLETED&lt;/code&gt;, without waiting for a manual console check.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Consistent handling across accounts.&lt;/strong&gt; The same AWS Lambda function and Amazon EventBridge rule pattern can be deployed for each organization that receives third-party model grants, so activation logic doesn’t vary by operator.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Built-in audit trail.&lt;/strong&gt; Every activation attempt or skip decision is logged to CloudWatch Logs, giving you a record of when each grant was activated, deferred, or ignored.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h4 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h4&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Before you deploy this path, confirm these prerequisites:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Use the AWS Organizations management account in &lt;code&gt;us-east-1&lt;/code&gt; with Python 3.12+, AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI), and AWS CDK v2 configured.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Confirm that AWS Organizations uses all features and that the private offer is for a License Manager-backed Bedrock model. Software as a service (SaaS) products are out of scope for this automation.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h4 id="implementation-steps"&gt;Implementation steps&lt;/h4&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Clone the sample repository locally and move into the project directory.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;git clone https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-managed-entitlements.git
cd sample-bedrock-managed-entitlements&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Verify or enable organization prerequisites.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python3 scripts/bootstrap_prereqs.py --check --region us-east-1&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;
  &lt;p&gt;Review any &lt;code&gt;APPLY&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;MANUAL&lt;/code&gt; items before making changes. The bootstrap script can enable License Manager organization integration, License Manager trusted access, AWS Marketplace license-management trusted access, and required service-linked roles.&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python3 scripts/bootstrap_prereqs.py --apply --region us-east-1 --confirm-account-id ACCOUNT_ID&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Configure deployment inputs.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python3 scripts/setup_config.py&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;
  &lt;p&gt;Review &lt;code&gt;config/sellers.json&lt;/code&gt;. Configure your Organization ID, seller allow-list, notifications, and optional grant targets. By default, grants target the full organization. Use &lt;code&gt;grantTargets&lt;/code&gt; to scope grants to selected OUs or AWS accounts.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Deploy the CDK stack.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cd cdk
pip install -r requirements.txt
cdk bootstrap aws://ACCOUNT_ID/us-east-1
cdk deploy&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Seed the Amazon DynamoDB seller allow-list from &lt;code&gt;config/sellers.json&lt;/code&gt;.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cd ..
python scripts/seed_sellers.py --config config/sellers.json&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;
  &lt;p&gt;Treat the seller allow-list as a security boundary. Verify each seller account ID against your accepted AWS Marketplace private offer or received License Manager license before seeding the table. Don’t add broad or unverified seller entries, because matching sellers can trigger grant creation and activation across your configured grant targets.&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;p&gt;After deployment and seller seeding, the stack is ready to process eligible AWS Marketplace agreement events. Grant creation and activation happen only for verified sellers in the allowlist, and activation may be deferred until License Manager finishes processing the grant workflow.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Validate the deployed automation.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python scripts/e2e_validate.py --org-id o-xxxxxxxxxx --seller-account SELLER_ACCOUNT_ID --simulate&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Verify grant activation after an eligible AWS Marketplace agreement event.
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws logs tail /aws/lambda/mppo-grants-handler --follow --region us-east-1
python scripts/bedrock_discount_check.py --issuer "ISV Partner"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;
  &lt;p&gt;Activation and billing visibility can take hours or days. After the event-driven workflow accepts and activates the grants, confirm the grant state in AWS License Manager, then validate covered Amazon Bedrock usage in AWS Cost Explorer after billing data appears. For a repeatable check, adapt the sample &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-managed-entitlements/blob/main/scripts/bedrock_discount_check.py"&gt;scripts/bedrock_discount_check.py&lt;/a&gt; utility to match your ISV Partner issuer and target linked account.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If you deployed this stack for testing, remove it to avoid the small ongoing Lambda and Amazon EventBridge charges:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cdk destroy&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="voice-of-customer"&gt;Voice of customer&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“As an AI Care Partner supporting millions of clinician consults every week, Heidi Health relies on third-party foundation models at real scale. Private pricing on AWS Marketplace gives us a cost-effective and efficient way to manage that growth: we can granularly attribute usage and pricing across AWS accounts and organizational units, and automate grant management as we adopt new models. That lets our platform team scale cost governance without scaling manual work.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;— &lt;a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/marcuslau35/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Marcus Lau&lt;/a&gt;, Head of Platform Engineering, Heidi Health&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This post showed two ways to activate managed entitlements for Amazon Bedrock private pricing offers. Use the lightweight script for one-time activation of trusted received licenses, or deploy the CDK automation when you need ongoing event-driven processing for new AWS Marketplace agreements. Both approaches reduce manual effort and help negotiated pricing apply after distributed grants are activated.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To get started, try the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-managed-entitlements" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;code sample&lt;/a&gt; in your AWS management account to automate model subscriptions.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="resources"&gt;Resources&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-managed-entitlements" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Sample code: Amazon Bedrock with managed entitlements (GitHub)&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/license-manager/latest/userguide/what-is-license-manager.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS License Manager User Guide&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/license-manager/latest/APIReference/API_CreateGrantVersion.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS License Manager API Reference: CreateGrantVersion&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/eventbridge/latest/userguide/what-is-amazon-eventbridge.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon EventBridge User Guide&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cdk/v2/guide/home.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CDK Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Natural language security queries at Orca Security using Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/natural-language-security-queries-at-orca-security-using-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Deniz Yilmaz]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 13 Oct 2026 16:03:31 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">ab143ebe8db2864cd1a1dc85aca1d8077f428d42</guid>

					<description>Orca Security built Discovery AI, a natural language query agent on Amazon Bedrock that converts plain-English questions into Sonar queries. The post shares the multi-stage architecture and the results: 90% lower query cost, 93% fewer input tokens, and 75% lower latency than the initial baseline.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Security analysts need fast answers about their cloud infrastructure without deep familiarity with domain-specific query languages. &lt;a href="https://orca.security/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Orca Security&lt;/a&gt;’s tool covers over 900 cloud models (the resource types Sonar tracks, such as an Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) instance or an Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) bucket). Querying that data required precise model names, nested relationship navigation, and selection from 18 operators (comparison functions like equals or contains used to filter results).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To solve this, Orca Security partnered with AWS to build Discovery AI, a natural language query agent on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;. Orca provides context-aware security for cloud, code, and AI, and Sonar is its domain-specific query system where users search, filter, and explore across the entire data graph.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we share the architecture and results of Discovery AI, which converts a natural language question into a Sonar query. As a result, Orca reduced query costs by 90 percent, input tokens by 93 percent, and latency by 75 percent compared to the initial baseline.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="bridging-natural-language-and-structured-queries"&gt;Bridging natural language and structured queries&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Orca Security’s Sonar query system supports 900+ cloud infrastructure and application models with complex relationships between them. To query this data, users construct JSON objects that specify exact model names, select from 18 operators, and navigate parent-child hierarchies across cloud providers. Doing this well requires deep familiarity with the Sonar schema.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To build this capability, the Orca team set out to build a natural language interface that can generate valid Sonar queries from plain text.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Their initial proof of concept loaded the full Sonar schema into a single prompt on the largest available model. This included over 900 model definitions, every relationship type, and a generous set of example queries. The output quality was strong (design partners and internal users responded positively) but three significant drawbacks emerged at production scale.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Token consumption was inefficient, at roughly 2 million input tokens per workload, which drove up cost. Latency was high, with end-to-end response times of around 60 seconds per workload. Accuracy on complex, multi-model queries was limited because nested relationships and multi-condition queries often required additional refinement.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The Orca team validated that the concept worked. The next challenge was making it cost-effective, fast, and accurate enough for production. To address this, the Orca team designed and deployed a solution that handles thousands of queries per day at 15-second response times using Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Orca designed Discovery AI as a hybrid solution on Amazon Bedrock that combines Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) with schema-aware query construction. The system converts natural language questions into valid Discovery filters through a multi-stage pipeline, with each stage matched to the most appropriate Amazon Bedrock model.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Orca chose Amazon Bedrock because it provides multi-model access through a single API, letting the team allocate different foundation models to each pipeline stage without managing separate integrations. Amazon Bedrock also inherits AWS governance controls through AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM), virtual private cloud (VPC) endpoints, and AWS CloudTrail, meeting Orca’s security requirements. The model-agnostic architecture meant the team could swap in newer model versions as they became available without rearchitecting the pipeline.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram illustrates the nine-stage pipeline architecture.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/05/ML-21130-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/05/ML-21130-1.png" alt="Nine-stage Discovery AI pipeline on Amazon Bedrock, from natural language input to a validated Discovery filter" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Multi-stage Discovery AI pipeline architecture showing the flow from natural language input to validated Discovery filter queries using foundation models on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The Discovery AI pipeline converts a natural language request into a validated Discovery filter through nine steps.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Request Entry and Context Setup: The pipeline starts by combining the user’s natural language request with relevant context, including the user’s organization, connected cloud providers, and request metadata. This context helps the pipeline interpret the request accurately and generate a Discovery filter that matches the customer’s environment.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Quick Deterministic Matcher: The Quick Matcher identifies structured inputs such as IP addresses, Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVEs), ARNs, file hashes, Amazon Machine Image IDs, Oracle Cloud Identifiers, EC2 IDs, URLs, and alert IDs. It uses regex and rule-based matching to detect high-confidence patterns.When a match is found, the pipeline can return a predefined Discovery filter through a fast path, without using large language model (LLM) inference. When no match is found, the request continues to the next step.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;LLM Guardrail Screening: The Guardrail component checks whether the request is safe, relevant, technical, and supported. It blocks prompt injection attempts, off-topic requests, and unsupported inputs before they reach the rest of the pipeline. Requests that fail the guardrail checks are rejected with an appropriate error. Approved requests continue to example retrieval.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Embedding-Based Semantic Candidate Recall: The pipeline searches for relevant previously validated query examples using Amazon Titan Text Embeddings. It retrieves the 20 nearest examples from the vector store based on semantic similarity. These candidates provide the pipeline with examples of how similar natural language requests were previously translated into Discovery filters.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Neural Relevance Reranking: The retrieved candidates are reranked using Cohere Rerank 3.5 on Amazon Bedrock. It identifies the examples that are most relevant to the current request and selects the five highest-signal examples. When an exact or high-confidence match is found, the pipeline can reuse the known-good filter directly. Otherwise, the reranked examples are passed to the generative stages.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;LLM-Driven Relevant Model Selection: Model Selection narrows more than 900 Sonar models to the small set relevant to the request. It considers the user’s request, connected providers, and retrieved examples when selecting the appropriate models. The selected models are provided to the Generator using the appropriate schema level, ranging from compact micro schemas to more detailed mini schemas. This reduces unnecessary context and makes generation more efficient.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Tool-Augmented Agentic Filter Generation: The Generator uses a tool-augmented agent to construct the final Discovery filter. It reasons over the selected model schemas, retrieved examples, user context, and available tools. The agent can iteratively refine the filter as it works toward a valid representation of the user’s request.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Static and API-Backed Validation: The generated filter is checked for syntactic and semantic correctness. The pipeline first applies static validation and then verifies the filter against Orca’s Sonar API. When validation fails, the validation error is returned to the Generator so the agent can correct the filter and try again. If the maximum number of retries is reached without producing a valid filter, the pipeline returns an invalid-filter response. The Generator is constrained to the reranked examples and selected schemas. Static validation resolves every model, field, and operator back to those schema definitions. Each validation error goes back to the agent with the offending element identified, so retries re-ground rather than re-guess.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Post-Processing and Response: After a valid filter is produced, whether through the Quick Matcher, reuse of a known-good example, or agentic generation, the pipeline prepares the final response.The team allocated different foundation models on Amazon Bedrock across components based on each stage’s requirements. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-card-anthropic-claude-haiku-4-5.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Anthropic Claude Haiku 4.5&lt;/a&gt; powers the Model Selector and Generator. These components require strong reasoning for accurate query construction. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-card-amazon-nova-2-lite.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova 2 Lite&lt;/a&gt; handles the Guardrail and Validator, where fast inference at lower cost is prioritized. Amazon Titan provides embeddings for semantic search in the Example Retrieval component. Cohere Rerank 3.5 reranks retrieved candidates to maximize relevance. For model availability by AWS Region, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-region-compatibility.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Regional availability by models&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;h2 id="maximizing-relevance-and-diversity-in-example-retrieval"&gt;Maximizing relevance and diversity in example retrieval&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Example retrieval emerged as the single biggest accuracy lever in the pipeline. Strong examples teach the Generator how to translate questions into Sonar queries more efficiently than schema alone, so the Orca team benchmarked several retrieval strategies on Amazon Bedrock. Each strategy traded off relevance, diversity, or speed in a different way, and comparing them directly showed which combination worked best.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Pure top-K semantic search on Amazon Titan embeddings performed quickly, but it tended to return near-duplicate examples that starved the Generator of diversity. Maximal Marginal Relevance (MMR) improved diversity, but it traded off relevance when the top candidate was actually the right answer. Retrieving a larger candidate set and then reranking it pulled in a broader pool and used a reranker to select the most relevant subset, which pointed toward the winning approach.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The winner was top-20 nearest-neighbor retrieval using Amazon Titan embeddings. Orca then reranked these results with Cohere Rerank 3.5 down to the top five. This approach gives the Generator a small, diverse, highly relevant set of examples without forcing a trade-off between coverage and precision at retrieval time.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="loading-900-models-into-context"&gt;Loading over 900 models into context&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;A core constraint shaped the architecture. It wasn’t feasible to include 900+ Sonar model schemas into every prompt. Even with long-context models, doing so degrades both cost and accuracy.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The Model Selector resolves this. Running on Anthropic Claude Haiku 4.5, it works against compact, semantically rich representations of each model (name, purpose, key fields, and key relationships). Given the user’s question, it returns only the models the Generator actually needs. The Generator then receives full schema detail only for those selected models plus the relationships that connect them.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="evaluation-methodology"&gt;Evaluation methodology&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To iterate confidently, the Orca team built a continuous evaluation harness early in the project. The harness combines a curated dataset, an automated scoring layer, and a slice-based measurement approach so the team can trust every change before it ships. Together these elements turn evaluation into a routine step rather than an afterthought.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The curated evaluation dataset spans the question types, model families, and complexity tiers seen in production, so it reflects real usage. An LLM-as-judge scoring layer grades generated queries on correctness, completeness, and semantic equivalence to a reference query, which gives the team a consistent quality signal. The measurement methodology then breaks deltas down by slice, allowing the team to identify exactly where regressions occur and what to correct.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Every change to a prompt, a model, a retrieval parameter, or an example flows through the harness before it ships to production.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="domain-expert-in-the-loop-labeling-system-for-continuous-improvement"&gt;Domain-expert-in-the-loop labeling system for continuous improvement&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Sustaining accuracy as customer environments evolve and new question patterns emerge required more than a strong initial deployment. The Orca team built an internal labeling tool for product managers, support engineers, and security researchers. With the tool, they can flag queries, correct outputs, and push corrections into the example bank in one action. Furthermore, the system automatically collects production queries with low-confidence matching examples and routes them to the appropriate domain expert for daily labeling. The next pipeline run retrieves the new example through Cohere Rerank 3.5, and accuracy on that class of question improves the same moment, with no deployment.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“The labeling UI is the single most important investment we made for long-term quality. It turns Discovery AI from a static deployment into a living system, with our domain experts continuously improving it inside their normal workflow.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;— Or Gabay, Director of AI, Orca Security&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Combined with end-to-end pipeline observability and shared dashboards spanning product, engineering, and customer-facing teams, this feedback loop is what keeps Discovery AI improving after initial launch.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After deploying Discovery AI to production, the new solution outperformed the initial baseline across cost, latency, and accuracy. Input token usage fell by 93 percent, from approximately 2 million tokens down to 140,000 per comparable workload. Overall cost fell by 90 percent, driven by that lower token consumption together with per-stage model allocation across Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Latency also improved, with end-to-end response times dropping by 75 percent, from approximately 60 seconds to 15 seconds. Accuracy on complex, multi-model queries also improved, measured continuously through the team’s evaluation harness.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Discovery AI now handles thousands of queries per day in production since March 2026, with internal subject matter experts and customer feedback validating quality.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we shared how Orca Security built Discovery AI on Amazon Bedrock. The solution reduced query costs by 90 percent, input token usage by 93 percent, and latency by 75 percent compared to the initial baseline. The solution combines several key technologies. These include RAG-based example retrieval with Cohere Rerank 3.5, per-stage model allocation across Anthropic Claude Haiku 4.5 and Amazon Nova 2 Lite, and semantic search powered by Amazon Titan. Additionally, a continuous human-in-the-loop labeling system provides ongoing improvement. This combination allowed Orca to turn a working prototype into a production-grade solution that is both cheaper to run and more accurate.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To get started building similar solutions, explore &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; for foundation model (FM) access, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/cohere/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cohere Rerank on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; for retrieval optimization, and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/titan-models.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Titan&lt;/a&gt; for embeddings. To learn more about Orca Security’s cloud security tool, visit &lt;a href="https://orca.security/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;orca.security&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To learn more about how Orca Security applies AI across its framework, see &lt;a href="https://orca.security/platform/orca-ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;how Orca drives security outcomes with AI&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://orca.security/platform/secure-ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;how Orca secures AI adoption, including Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For more on the techniques used in this post, read &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/text-to-sql-solution-powered-by-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;related posts&lt;/a&gt; on the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Machine Learning Blog&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
