			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How ShipStation Global built an AI IT help desk on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-shipstation-global-built-an-ai-it-help-desk-on-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Kenneth Salmon]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 13 Oct 2026 15:54:49 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">925aef534e91c4fa81028a17497d83107c32ab6e</guid>

					<description>ShipStation Global's engineering team built Deckhand, an AI IT help desk agent for Slack on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. It answers routine IT questions from a curated knowledge base, reads error-message screenshots, and files Jira tickets. In its first three months it resolved 97.5% of channel queries with a 96% positive feedback rating.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This post is co-written with Kenneth Salmon, Edward Boswell, Jack Armstrong, Robert Banh, and Nick Ciubotariu from ShipStation Global.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Answering the same IT questions and clearing routine tickets drains time that IT teams could spend elsewhere. To reclaim it, the ShipStation Global Engineering team built &lt;em&gt;Deckhand&lt;/em&gt;, an AI-powered IT help desk agent for Slack, on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.shipstationglobal.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ShipStation Global&lt;/a&gt; is the leading intelligent shipping and logistics platform for small and mid-sized shippers, and it operates on a simple belief: the more efficient its teams are, the better it delivers for customers. Deckhand puts that belief to work. It answers routine IT and company questions from a curated knowledge base, reads error-message screenshots, and creates and updates Jira Service Management tickets.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we discuss how ShipStation architected Deckhand, which resulted in resolving 97.5 percent of incoming channel queries while earning a 96 percent positive feedback rating by employees (Figure 1).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-21795-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-21795-1.png" alt="Chart showing Deckhand reached 97.5% channel coverage and a 96% positive feedback rating over three months" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: 3-month channel coverage and positive feedback&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="business-challenge"&gt;Business challenge&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The problem Deckhand solves is the one every IT team will recognize: the same questions are routinely asked, error messages are posted after business hours, and information needs to be gathered from different teams. At ShipStation Global, those conversations lived in a busy help desk Slack channel, where employees asked, and IT answered, one thread at a time. The answers usually already existed in a Confluence how-to article, on a company intranet page, or in a thread from eight months earlier. Finding them was the hard part.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The channel data made the scale concrete. In the 12 months before launch:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Employees posted more than 1,500 threads, and volume kept growing.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;With employees in the US, UK, Spain, Poland, Australia, and the Philippines, more than a fifth of questions arrived outside US business hours.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Answers were split across different places: Confluence articles, intranet pages and PDFs, IT-maintained guides, and the channel’s own history.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Repetitive questions consumed time that structured self-service steps could have handled.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“We created Deckhand because getting help should be as simple as asking a question in Slack, not deciphering a ticket form or sending an email into the void. We believe that tickets are an indication of operational friction not productivity. Deckhand resolves common questions on the spot and, when it can’t, converts the conversation into a fully contextualized ticket with one click, improving the employee experience and freeing our team from endlessly repeating the phrase: ‘Please file a ticket.’”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;— Chris Collins, Senior Director IT at ShipStation Global&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt;
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Deckhand works in Slack like any teammate—joining channels, reading the conversation, and drawing context from Slack profiles to answer IT questions in place. We run it on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, hosts the agent in isolated sessions, so we run no servers or containers.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The solution has four main components, shown in Figure 2:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Slack service:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;A FastAPI and Slack Bolt application, hosted on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/apprunner/latest/dg/what-is-apprunner.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS App Runner&lt;/a&gt;. It receives Slack events, decides whether the bot should respond, and builds a user-context payload from the requester’s Slack profile, including name, email, time zone, and manager.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent service:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;A Strands agent, deployed as a container on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime-how-it-works.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime&lt;/a&gt;, manages the multi-turn tool agent loop using Anthropic’s Claude in &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/what-is-bedrock.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tools and knowledge:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;A single &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/lambda/latest/dg/welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Lambda&lt;/a&gt; function, exposed as Model Context Protocol (MCP) tools through &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-core-concepts.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, classifies requests, creates and updates Jira tickets, and analyzes images. An &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/knowledge-base.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock knowledge base&lt;/a&gt; indexes content from Confluence, the company intranet, Slack, and marketing pages, storing it in &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/Welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Operations and feedback.&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/Introduction.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon DynamoDB&lt;/a&gt; stores feedback, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/xray/latest/devguide/aws-xray.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS X-Ray&lt;/a&gt; traces request paths using OpenTelemetry, and the services log to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonCloudWatch/latest/logs/WhatIsCloudWatchLogs.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-21795-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-21795-2.png" alt="Architecture diagram of Deckhand on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore showing the Slack service, agent runtime, knowledge base, Gateway-backed Lambda tools, Jira Service Management, and DynamoDB feedback" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Architecture diagram showing Slack events entering the Slack service and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime, with separate paths to an Amazon Bedrock knowledge base, AgentCore Gateway-backed Lambda tools, Jira Service Management, and DynamoDB feedback.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;When an employee posts in a channel where Deckhand is a member, the Slack service assembles the full thread history and user context. It then invokes the agent through the AgentCore runtime API. The agent searches the knowledge base and calls tools as needed. When it finds an answer, it returns a final response (Figure 3). When it doesn’t, it asks a clarifying question or offers to create a ticket (Figure 4).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 500px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-21795-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-21795-3.png" alt="Slack screenshot of Deckhand replying with numbered self-service steps" width="490"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Deckhand responds with self-service steps&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-21795-4.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-21795-4.jpg" alt="Slack screenshot of Deckhand offering a Create Ticket button" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Deckhand offers to create a ticket&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="deckhand-workflow"&gt;Deckhand workflow&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This section walks through how Deckhand is implemented, from prerequisites to a working deployment.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To implement this pattern, you need the following:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with access to Amazon Bedrock, AgentCore runtime, and AgentCore Gateway.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;A Slack app with event subscriptions, a signing secret, and a bot token scoped to your use case.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;A container image registry such as Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An Amazon S3 bucket and an Amazon Bedrock knowledge base.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) roles scoped to each runtime and tool, and AWS Secrets Manager entries for third-party credentials.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h3 id="building-the-agent-with-strands-agents-and-agentcore"&gt;Building the agent with Strands Agents and AgentCore&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The agent is intentionally lightweight. Using the open source &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/opensource/introducing-strands-agents-an-open-source-ai-agents-sdk/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents SDK&lt;/a&gt;, an agent consists of a model, a set of tools, and a system prompt. The SDK manages the reasoning loop, allowing the model to select tools, process results, and iterate until it produces an answer. Deckhand’s agent service is implemented in a few lines of Python.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from strands import Agent
from strands.models import BedrockModel
from strands.tools.mcp import MCPClient
from strands_tools import retrieve
from mcp.client.streamable_http import streamablehttp_client

model = BedrockModel(
    model_id=MODEL_ID,
    max_tokens=2048,
    temperature=0.3,
)

gateway = MCPClient(lambda: streamablehttp_client(url=GATEWAY_URL))

agent = Agent(
    model=model,
    tools=[gateway, retrieve],
    system_prompt=SYSTEM_PROMPT,
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The model ID is set through an environment variable, which allows switching models without modifying application code.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Tools are integrated with the agent in two ways. Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases retrieval is a native Strands tool that supports knowledge base search with a single import. Other functions, including classification, ticket management, user resolution, and image analysis, are handled by a Lambda function registered on AgentCore Gateway. The Gateway exposes these as MCP tools, and the Lambda routes each invocation by tool name. This separation allows tool logic to be updated independently, and the agent can access new tools from the Gateway without requiring a rebuild.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore runtime hosts the agent as a container, avoiding the need for server or session management. The Slack service calls the agent synchronously for each message, providing the thread transcript and user context, and posts the final response. Since context, including the employee’s profile, is included with every request, the agent can complete ticket details automatically.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="knowledge-base-design-and-answer-routing"&gt;Knowledge base design and answer routing&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Deckhand answers from a single Amazon Bedrock knowledge base with core S3-backed data sources:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Articles synced from Confluence.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Internal pages, PDFs, and events synced from the company intranet.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Resolved support threads mined from Slack (covered in the next section).&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Each document includes metadata such as title, source URL, content type, and event dates. This allows the agent to filter content, for example, when users ask about upcoming events or weekly lunch menus in the Austin office.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="mining-slack-history-with-recursive-language-models-rlms"&gt;Mining Slack history with Recursive Language Models (RLMs)&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The most valuable data source is the archive of resolved support threads. Before Deckhand existed, Slack was where problems got solved: years of employees describing issues and IT walking them through fixes. That history is an organic knowledge base, validated by the best possible reviewer: the person whose problem got fixed. It’s also noisy: a raw export mixes banter, automated alerts, and threads that never actually got resolved. Indexing an unverified fix or a garbled summary means the knowledge base surfaces an incorrect answer with the same confidence as a correct one, so extraction needs to be precise.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The mining process follows a three-stage pipeline (Figure 5):&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;A script exports the channel’s full history via the Slack API, retrieving complete replies for every thread and writing a structured dump that includes thread markers, timestamps, and reply counts.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;DSPy’s Recursive Language Model (RLM), running Claude on Amazon Bedrock, treats that export as a navigable memory space inside a sandboxed Python read-eval-print loop (REPL) rather than a single prompt. Working iteratively over the data, it classifies each thread as resolved, unresolved, or escalated and filters out announcements and non-questions. Strict criteria keep quality high: a thread counts as resolved only when the full, workable fix is visible in the thread itself.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;For each resolved thread, the RLM orchestrates recursive sub-LLM (large language model) calls to extract structured fields, each validated against a Pydantic model. The fields include an issue summary, the original question, and the resolution. They also capture whether the fix is self-service or needs a ticket, the self-service steps, the application and system involved, error messages, and tags. When a sub-LLM call returns something that fails the schema, the RLM’s code-based loop catches the validation error in the interpreter and retries. The results are written as a markdown document organized by issue. The pipeline uploads it to Amazon S3 for the knowledge base to index.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-21795-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-21795-5.png" alt="Three-stage pipeline diagram: export Slack channel history, classify threads as resolved or not, and extract structured fields" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Export classification and extraction workflow of the Slack conversation&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;We chose &lt;a href="https://dspy.ai/diving-deeper/rlm" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;RLM&lt;/a&gt; because it maintains the entire Slack history in a persistent sandbox variable space, allowing iterative reasoning over the export and consistent output structure. As a result, when an employee encounters a problem previously solved by a colleague, Deckhand provides the proven solution, often as actionable, numbered steps.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="knowing-when-not-to-answer"&gt;Knowing when not to answer&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;An AI bot in a shared channel fails socially before it fails technically. Deckhand’s most refined logic decides when to stay quiet:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The agent’s first decision on every message is whether it’s an announcement, a social message, or a conversation between people. If so, it outputs “SKIP,” and the Slack service suppresses the reply.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;In threads, it replies only to the original poster.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;If the last reply came from another human helper, it stays silent, because a person has taken over.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;If a reply starts by &lt;span class="citation" data-cites="naming"&gt;@naming&lt;/span&gt; a person, the message isn’t for the bot.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;h3 id="collecting-feedback"&gt;Collecting feedback&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Each answer includes feedback buttons. A thumbs-up is recorded directly, while a thumbs-down prompts a brief “what went wrong” field. Ratings are stored in DynamoDB with the original question and the bot’s response.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Negative feedback is a valuable dataset used to improve the system prompt and agent behavior, and to update incorrect knowledge base information.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="security-and-validation"&gt;Security and validation&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Prompt rules enhance behavior but do not replace access controls. Enforcement is managed through code and infrastructure:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Ticket creation requires explicit confirmation. When Deckhand offers to create a ticket, the Slack service adds a &lt;strong&gt;Create Ticket&lt;/strong&gt; button, and selecting it sends confirmation through the agent. Ticket updates only accept valid keys matching the helpdesk project pattern and reject empty comments.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Classification is validated before it acts. Request classification runs as a separate &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/APIReference/API_runtime_Converse.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Converse API&lt;/a&gt; call that returns structured ticket fields, validated with Pydantic before anything reaches Jira.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The image tool is restricted to downloading only from Slack’s file host. It authenticates with the bot token, limits each request to five images of up to 5 MB each, and sends the files to a vision model using the Converse API.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Credentials stay out of code. Slack tokens, the signing secret, and Jira credentials live in &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/secretsmanager/latest/userguide/intro.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Secrets Manager&lt;/a&gt;, and each service and tool runs under its own scoped IAM role.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The prompt enforces behavioral safeguards: never request passwords or multi-factor authentication (MFA) codes, never write credentials in ticket comments, and never expose profile metadata used for ticket fields.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="outcome"&gt;Outcome&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Deckhand launched in April 2026. It started at about 30 messages a day and reached as many as 100 by early summer. In its first three months it handled 1,935 messages, started 1,221 agent conversations, and opened 409 Jira tickets. It replied to 97.5 percent of messages. A third of the questions came in after hours, when no one on the IT team was working, and Deckhand answered 94 percent of those. People who would have waited until the next morning got an answer that night.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Feedback improved as the team worked on it. Positive ratings went from 82 percent in April to 96 percent in June, after they revised the system prompt and knowledge base to fix what people complained about.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The clearest win was ticket deflection. An agent conversation is a question that would otherwise have become a Jira ticket. Of the 1,221 conversations, only 409 turned into tickets. The other 812 were handled in chat. Filing a ticket and then working and closing it runs about 30 minutes. Those 812 conversations add up to roughly 406 hours over three months, or around 1,600 hours a year. Employees kept that time for their own work, and IT never spent it on intake.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Deckhand took repetitive IT questions off people’s plates and gave the time back to employees and the IT team. It works because each part of the stack has one job and runs as a managed service: AgentCore runtime hosts the agent, AgentCore Gateway turns a Lambda function into MCP tools the agent can call, and Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases handles retrieval. The team shipped it without standing up servers or maintaining orchestration code.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To build a similar solution, begin with a well-maintained knowledge source and a clearly defined action. Refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime-how-it-works.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/opensource/introducing-strands-agents-an-open-source-ai-agents-sdk/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents SDK&lt;/a&gt; for agent development, and the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/knowledge-base.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases documentation&lt;/a&gt; for connecting content sources.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To discuss an agentic AI use case for your organization, contact your AWS account team.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Build long-horizon agents with Grok 4.7 on Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-long-horizon-agents-with-grok-4-7-on-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Suheel Farooq]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 13 Oct 2026 15:52:20 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Machine Learning]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Artificial Intelligence]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Foundation models]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Generative AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">57fb1aeaa95150736b7378dbd28aa9e97e0d4632</guid>

					<description>Grok 4.7 is now available on Amazon Bedrock. In this post, we build a tool-calling agent on Grok 4.7 and evaluate it on three reasoning tasks at four reasoning effort levels, measuring correctness and token cost per completed task. We show how to tune the trade-off per request and monitor it in production.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;SpaceXAI’s Grok 4.7 is now available on Amazon Bedrock, which offers a choice of foundation models (FMs) from multiple providers. According to SpaceXAI, Grok 4.7 is trained to work longer on more difficult tasks and knows when to check its own work. That matters most for agents, which carry a task through many steps and must verify what they find along the way.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;An agent that works this way has to do more than look things up. It has to form a candidate answer, check it against what it has learned, and refine it until it fits. Grok 4.7 exposes a reasoning effort setting that controls how much deliberation happens before each response, and for agent workloads a practical question is which level to run.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we build a tool-calling agent on Grok 4.7 and evaluate it on three tasks that require search rather than lookup. The tasks are diagnosing a two-service outage from its alerts, scheduling maintenance under interacting constraints, and choosing an optimal set of migrations under a budget. Each task has exactly one correct answer, verified by brute force. We ran all three at every reasoning effort level, six times each, for 72 agent runs.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The agent answered 67 of the 72 runs correctly. At &lt;code&gt;xhigh&lt;/code&gt; it answered all 18 correctly, and at &lt;code&gt;low&lt;/code&gt; it answered 15 of 18 while using about an eighth of the output tokens. That gives a clear trade-off you can tune per request, and we show how to measure it on your own tasks and how to monitor it in production.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;We report token counts rather than currency throughout. Token prices change, and the counts are what your own costs are derived from, so a measurement expressed in tokens stays valid.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="what-spacexai-reports-about-grok-4.7"&gt;What SpaceXAI reports about Grok 4.7&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;According to SpaceXAI, Grok 4.7 uses a larger base model than Grok 4.6 and was trained with a longer reinforcement learning run on a harder mix of tasks. This mix is weighted toward problems that take many hours to complete. SpaceXAI reports that the model is better at verifying its own work and at managing longer context.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;SpaceXAI’s published benchmark results follow that emphasis. The largest reported gains over Grok 4.6 appear in the categories that measure sustained work rather than single-turn answers.&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;p&gt;Source: SpaceXAI, Introducing Grok 4.7 (&lt;a class="uri" href="https://x.ai/news/grok-4-7" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;https://x.ai/news/grok-4-7&lt;/a&gt;). Scores are as published by SpaceXAI at the time of the model launch.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Benchmarks show how a model performs across standard tasks. For your own workload, the useful next step is to measure on tasks shaped like yours, which is what the rest of this post walks through.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;SpaceXAI lists a 500K token context window for Grok 4.7, with text and image input and text output. On Amazon Bedrock, the model supports configurable reasoning effort, tool calling, and structured output.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="how-you-access-grok-4.7-on-amazon-bedrock"&gt;How you access Grok 4.7 on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Grok 4.7 runs on the &lt;code&gt;bedrock-runtime&lt;/code&gt; endpoint and is accessed through a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cross-region-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;cross-Region inference&lt;/a&gt; profile. Two profiles are available:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Inference profile&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Routing&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use when&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;us.xai.grok-4.7&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Within the US geography&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;You have US data residency requirements&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;global.xai.grok-4.7&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Commercial AWS Regions&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;You have no residency constraints and want the widest capacity&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Cross-Region inference routes requests across multiple AWS Regions, which gives you higher throughput without managing capacity Region by Region. Requests name one of the inference profiles rather than a bare model ID.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The model supports the OpenAI-compatible Chat Completions and Responses APIs, and the Amazon Bedrock Converse API. On Converse, reasoning is returned as a &lt;code&gt;reasoningContent&lt;/code&gt; block alongside the &lt;code&gt;text&lt;/code&gt; block. In &lt;code&gt;inferenceConfig&lt;/code&gt;, set &lt;code&gt;maxTokens&lt;/code&gt; only, and put model-specific parameters, such as reasoning effort, in &lt;code&gt;additionalModelRequestFields&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For the current list of supported Regions, service tiers, and token prices, see the Grok 4.7 model card in the Amazon Bedrock User Guide and the Amazon Bedrock pricing page (&lt;a class="uri" href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with access to Amazon Bedrock, and model access enabled for Grok 4.7.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.9 or later, with &lt;code&gt;openai&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;boto3&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;aws-bedrock-token-generator&lt;/code&gt; installed.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;AWS credentials configured in your environment.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Your AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) identity needs &lt;code&gt;bedrock:InvokeModel&lt;/code&gt; on both the inference profile and your account’s default project:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;arn:aws:bedrock:{region}::inference-profile/us.xai.grok-4.7&lt;/code&gt; or the &lt;code&gt;global.&lt;/code&gt; equivalent.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;arn:aws:bedrock:{region}:{account-id}:project/default&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Include both resources in the policy statement. The default project permission is required in addition to the inference profile.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="connect-to-the-model"&gt;Connect to the model&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For production use, we recommend generating a short-term bearer token from your existing AWS credentials, so authentication stays tied to your IAM identity and no long-lived secret is stored:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from aws_bedrock_token_generator import provide_token
from openai import OpenAI

REGION = "us-west-2"
MODEL_ID = "us.xai.grok-4.7"

client = OpenAI(
    api_key=provide_token(region=REGION),
    base_url=f"https://bedrock-runtime.{REGION}.amazonaws.com/openai/v1",
    timeout=360,
    max_retries=0,  # retries are handled explicitly, see "Build in retries" below
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Reasoning tokens count as output tokens and draw down &lt;code&gt;max_completion_tokens&lt;/code&gt; before the answer text is produced, so size the limit to leave room for both. If the limit is reached during reasoning, the response returns without answer text, so check for empty content in agent code and raise the limit if you see it. Our runs used a limit of 32,768 tokens, which left headroom at every effort level.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Grok 4.7 supports four effort levels: &lt;code&gt;low&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;medium&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;high&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;xhigh&lt;/code&gt;. Effort levels can change between model versions, so it is worth confirming them against the service rather than hardcoding a list:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def supported_effort_levels(candidates=("none", "low", "medium", "high", "xhigh", "max")):
    supported = []
    for level in candidates:
        try:
            client.responses.create(model=MODEL_ID, input="Say OK.",
                reasoning={"effort": level}, max_output_tokens=2048)
            supported.append(level)
        except Exception as exc:
            if "unsupported_value" not in str(exc) and "invalid_value" not in str(exc):
                raise
    return supported


print(supported_effort_levels())  # ['low', 'medium', 'high', 'xhigh']&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="design-tasks-that-need-reasoning"&gt;Design tasks that need reasoning&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Many agent evaluations test diligence: read a record, filter it, add it up. Those tasks are worth having, but to see what reasoning effort contributes, a task has to require search: the answer can’t be read off the data, it has to be proposed and then checked.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;We built a synthetic environment of 20 services, exposed through seven tools, and three tasks on top of it. Every task has exactly one correct answer, and each is built so that a quick heuristic is not enough to find it.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Task&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What the agent has to do&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What makes it a reasoning task&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;two_fault_diagnosis&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Two services failed silently at 03:00 and 12 others raised alerts. Identify the two that failed&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Failed services raise no alerts, so the answer is not among the alerting services. 190 possible pairs, and only one explains exactly the alerts observed&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;maintenance_schedule&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Patch eight services over four nights, two per night, under ten constraints and a rule that two services from the same team never share a night&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;2,520 possible schedules, one valid. The team rule depends on data the agent has to look up&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;migration_budget&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Choose candidates to migrate that maximize risk reduction within a budget, including transitive prerequisites&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;53 feasible sets, one optimal. Picking the best ratio first gives a set nine points short of the optimum&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Four practices keep a measurement like this trustworthy, and they apply to most agent evaluations.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;State every rule the grader relies on.&lt;/strong&gt; If the prompt leaves an edge case open, a reasonable answer can be scored as wrong, and the evaluation ends up measuring the specification rather than the model. The diagnosis prompt, for example, says explicitly that a failed service raises no alert of its own.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prove each task has one answer.&lt;/strong&gt; Each environment is small enough to enumerate, so we checked every candidate answer before running anything. For the schedule, that is a few lines:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import itertools

ALL_SLOTS = sorted(set(itertools.permutations([1, 1, 2, 2, 3, 3, 4, 4])))

def valid_schedules(services, constraints):
    return [s for slots in ALL_SLOTS
        if satisfies(s := dict(zip(services, slots)), constraints)]

assert len(valid_schedules(SERVICES, CONSTRAINTS)) == 1  # exactly one answer
assert len(valid_schedules(SERVICES, CONSTRAINTS_WITHOUT_TEAM_RULE)) &amp;gt; 1  # the rule matters&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Grade a required final line, not the whole response.&lt;/strong&gt; Each prompt asks the agent to end with a line such as &lt;code&gt;FINAL: auth, checkout&lt;/code&gt;. The grader reads only that line, so a response that discusses other candidates while explaining its reasoning is graded on its conclusion:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def final_line(answer):
    lines = [l for l in (answer or "").splitlines() if "FINAL" in l.upper()]
    return lines[-1] if lines else None&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Track format separately from correctness.&lt;/strong&gt; A missing final line is a different signal from an incorrect one. In our runs, all 72 responses included a gradeable final line.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Constrain tool parameters with an &lt;code&gt;enum&lt;/code&gt; where the valid values are known, so every tool call targets a real resource:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;TOOLS = [
    {"type": "function", "function": {
        "name": "get_dependencies",
        "description": "Services that one service directly depends on.",
        "parameters": {
            "type": "object",
            "properties": {"service": {"type": "string", "enum": SERVICE_NAMES}},
            "required": ["service"],
        }}},
    # The remaining six tools follow the same shape.
]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The system prompt is deliberately short, and asks the agent to check its answer before giving it:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;AGENT_SYSTEM = (
    "You are an infrastructure analyst agent. Use the tools to gather facts and never guess "
    "a value you can look up. Before answering, check your answer against every rule in the "
    "task. End with the FINAL line exactly as the task specifies."
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The agent loop is a standard tool-calling loop: call the model with &lt;code&gt;reasoning_effort&lt;/code&gt; set, execute each returned tool call, append the result keyed by &lt;code&gt;tool_call_id&lt;/code&gt;, and repeat until the model stops requesting tools. Tracking &lt;code&gt;prompt_tokens&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;completion_tokens&lt;/code&gt; across the loop gives the token counts reported in the following section.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Six runs of each task at each effort level, 72 runs in total. The agent answered 67 correctly.&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22076-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22076-1-1.png" alt="Bar charts showing correctness rate and mean output tokens per run across low, medium, high, and xhigh reasoning effort levels" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Share of runs answered correctly at each reasoning effort level (left), and mean output tokens per run with median seconds per run overlaid (right)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Three observations.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;xhigh&lt;/code&gt; answered every run correctly.&lt;/strong&gt; All 18 runs across the three tasks were correct at &lt;code&gt;xhigh&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;medium&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;high&lt;/code&gt; answered 17 of 18 each. With six runs per task and level, the differences between levels are indicative rather than statistically conclusive, and the direction was consistent across all three tasks.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Effort changes how much the agent reasons, not how much it explores.&lt;/strong&gt; Tool calls were essentially flat, between 17.2 and 18.7 per run at every level. The agent gathered the same facts regardless of effort, and what changed was how much reasoning it applied to them, with output tokens rising from 1,184 at &lt;code&gt;low&lt;/code&gt; to 9,147 at &lt;code&gt;xhigh&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;low&lt;/code&gt; offers a strong cost-to-quality ratio.&lt;/strong&gt; At &lt;code&gt;low&lt;/code&gt;, the agent answered 15 of 18 runs correctly using about an eighth of the output tokens of &lt;code&gt;xhigh&lt;/code&gt;, with a median run of 18 seconds. Where a slightly lower completion rate is acceptable, or where answers are reviewed downstream, &lt;code&gt;low&lt;/code&gt; is an efficient choice. Where every answer needs to be right first time, higher levels are the safer default.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="how-effort-level-shapes-the-answer"&gt;How effort level shapes the answer&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The tasks were designed around a specific kind of difficulty, and looking at how each effort level handled them clarifies when the extra reasoning is worth it.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Optimization.&lt;/strong&gt; The migration task rewards considering combinations rather than ranking options one at a time. Selecting the candidates with the best individual ratios produces a reasonable answer that is nine points short of the optimum. At &lt;code&gt;medium&lt;/code&gt; and above, the agent found the optimal set in all 18 runs.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Hypothesis testing.&lt;/strong&gt; The diagnosis task requires checking a candidate pair in both directions: every alert has to be explained, and every service that stayed silent has to be consistent with the answer. At &lt;code&gt;medium&lt;/code&gt; and above, the agent identified the correct pair in all 18 runs.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Constraint satisfaction.&lt;/strong&gt; The scheduling task combines ten listed constraints with a team rule that depends on looked-up ownership data. &lt;code&gt;xhigh&lt;/code&gt; produced the valid schedule in every run, and this was the task where the highest level made the clearest difference.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The pattern across the three is that additional reasoning effort is most valuable where the agent has to weigh several candidate answers against each other, rather than follow a single path to the result.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="monitoring-reasoning-in-production"&gt;Monitoring reasoning in production&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In production you don’t have a known answer to grade against, but you do know how many output tokens and tool calls each run used. Those give you a useful operational signal.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Correct runs at each level stay above a clear floor of reasoning volume. Every correct run at &lt;code&gt;medium&lt;/code&gt; and above used at least 1,739 output tokens, and the typical run used several thousand. A run that makes no tool calls, or that produces far less output than is normal for its level, is worth flagging for review:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def flag_for_review(run, baseline):
    """baseline: output tokens of graded, correct runs at the same effort level."""
    reasons = []
    if run["tool_calls"] == 0:
        reasons.append("made no tool calls")
    if run["output_tokens"] &amp;lt; 0.2 * min(baseline):
        reasons.append(f"used {run['output_tokens']} output tokens, "
            f"under a fifth of the lowest correct run ({min(baseline)})")
    return reasons&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Calibrate the baseline from runs you have graded, per task and per effort level, then apply it to runs you have not. Treat a flag as a prompt to inspect or re-run. At &lt;code&gt;low&lt;/code&gt;, where every run is brief, output volume is a weaker signal, so pair it with spot checks.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Asking the agent to verify its answer in the system prompt is worth keeping, because it makes the agent’s reasoning straightforward to follow when you review a run. Pair it with automated grading wherever a known answer exists, so that quality is measured independently of the transcript.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="choosing-a-reasoning-effort"&gt;Choosing a reasoning effort&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;These results suggest a few practical rules.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Match effort to the kind of task.&lt;/strong&gt; Tasks that involve search, optimization, or testing a hypothesis benefit most from &lt;code&gt;medium&lt;/code&gt; or higher. Tasks that follow a single path, or whose answers are reviewed downstream, can run efficiently at &lt;code&gt;low&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Set effort per request, not per agent.&lt;/strong&gt; Effort is a request parameter, so an agent can run its planning and verification steps at a higher level and its mechanical steps, such as formatting a tool result, at &lt;code&gt;low&lt;/code&gt;. That combines much of the reliability of a higher level with a lower token cost.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Measure on your own tasks, with repeats.&lt;/strong&gt; A single run per configuration gives a single observation. Repeating each configuration several times shows how consistent it is.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Monitor reasoning volume.&lt;/strong&gt; Output tokens and tool calls per run, tracked by effort level, give you an early signal to review a run before its result is used.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="limitations-of-this-measurement"&gt;Limitations of this measurement&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Six runs per task and effort level.&lt;/strong&gt; Enough to show a consistent direction across three tasks, not enough for a confidence interval. Treat the differences between levels as indicative.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A synthetic environment.&lt;/strong&gt; The tools read from data generated in code, which is what makes it possible to prove each task has exactly one answer and to grade automatically. Real tools add latency, partial results and errors, none of which are tested here.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Minutes, not hours.&lt;/strong&gt; Median run time ranged from 18 seconds at &lt;code&gt;low&lt;/code&gt; to 106 seconds at &lt;code&gt;xhigh&lt;/code&gt;. This is a scaled-down proxy for long-horizon work. The method transfers. The absolute figures do not.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;One model version, one Region, one prompt per task.&lt;/strong&gt; Different phrasings of the same task may change the results, which is part of why stating every rule explicitly matters.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Token counts, not costs.&lt;/strong&gt; To convert to currency, apply the current rates for your inference profile and service tier from the Amazon Bedrock pricing page. The Global and US Geo profiles are priced differently, and the Flex and Priority service tiers apply multipliers.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="operational-controls-for-agent-workloads"&gt;Operational controls for agent workloads&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Build in retries.&lt;/strong&gt; Agents that issue a high volume of calls should treat retries as part of the design. Set a request timeout, retry only errors that are retryable, back off exponentially with jitter, and honor a &lt;code&gt;Retry-After&lt;/code&gt; header when one is returned:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import random, time

RETRYABLE = {408, 429, 500, 502, 503, 504}

def call_with_backoff(fn, attempts=7, base=4.0, cap=180.0):
    for attempt in range(1, attempts + 1):
        try:
            return fn()
        except Exception as exc:
            status = getattr(exc, "status_code", None)
            retryable = status in RETRYABLE or "timed out" in str(exc).lower()
            if not retryable or attempt == attempts:
                raise
            time.sleep(random.uniform(base, min(cap, base * 2 ** attempt)))&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;On-demand throughput scales up over time, so ramp a new high-volume workload gradually rather than starting at full rate.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Auditing a run.&lt;/strong&gt; Model invocation logging writes request and response data to Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) or Amazon CloudWatch Logs. This gives you the full transcript of runs you want to review after the fact.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Monitoring reasoning volume at scale.&lt;/strong&gt; Amazon CloudWatch metrics give you the aggregate view of token usage, which is where the per-level baselines described earlier become something you can alarm on.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Attributing spend across agents.&lt;/strong&gt; Cost itemization in AWS Cost Explorer and the AWS Cost and Usage Report breaks usage out per model, which combined with per-task token counts gives you both unit economics and the aggregate bill.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Matching cost to urgency.&lt;/strong&gt; Service tiers are set per request, so one deployment can send an overnight analysis through the Flex tier and an interactive request through Priority. For a workload that runs at a higher effort level, tier selection is a further lever on cost.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Choosing a routing profile.&lt;/strong&gt; Cross-Region inference widens the capacity pool a long run draws from. The Global profile serves from your choice of commercial AWS Region and is typically priced lower per token than geographic routing. The US profile keeps processing within the United States. Choose based on your data residency requirements as well as cost.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The examples in this post do not create billable AWS resources beyond per-request token usage. If you generated a long-term Amazon Bedrock API key, delete it in the Amazon Bedrock console when you are finished, because it remains valid until revoked. Short-term tokens generated from IAM credentials expire on their own.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If you enabled model invocation logging to Amazon S3 while testing, review the bucket and remove objects you no longer need.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;SpaceXAI trained Grok 4.7 to work longer on more difficult tasks and to know when to check its own work. On three tasks that required search rather than lookup, the agent answered 67 of 72 runs correctly, and every run at &lt;code&gt;xhigh&lt;/code&gt;. Because reasoning effort is set per request, you can choose where each step of an agent sits on the trade-off between thoroughness and token cost.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Three things are worth carrying into your own evaluation. Design tasks with one provable answer and state every rule the grader depends on. Repeat runs, so you see how consistent each configuration is. And monitor output tokens and tool calls by effort level, so you have an operational signal alongside your quality measurements.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Mixture-of-Experts kernel engineering on Amazon SageMaker AI</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/mixture-of-experts-kernel-engineering-on-amazon-sagemaker-ai/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Mona Mona]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 13 Oct 2026 15:17:09 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">a36fde525cae2db63b1b86626409e8ab9462e559</guid>

					<description>AWS engineers share kernel optimizations for fused Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models on Amazon SageMaker AI: Swap-AB for WGMMA utilization, Triton backend routing for FP8 quantization, and eliminating Triton JIT recompilation. Together they deliver up to 10% higher throughput and up to 9% faster Time to First Token on H200 GPUs.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) is a sparsely-activated architecture that scales large language model (LLM) capacity while keeping per-token compute low. Because its operations account for a large share of total compute time, inefficiencies there directly raise request latency and cost. Therefore, optimizing MoE operations through kernel engineering offers the largest opportunity for performance gains.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Fused MoE is a key optimization technique for the MoE architecture. A fused MoE system merges token routing, data shuffling, and specialized network calculations into a single batched operation on the GPU. This replaces executing those routing and expert computations as separate, sequential loop operations. While this provides latency advantages, it also brings complexity in kernel implementations and debugging capability. High GPU utilization for this architecture requires hardware-specific optimizations.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we describe the different kernel optimizations we implemented for fused MoE models and show you how you can benefit from this work. We used a representative MoE model as our primary example.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="swap-ab-for-wgmma-utilization"&gt;Swap-AB for WGMMA utilization&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To maximize tensor processing throughput, the NVIDIA Hopper architecture relies on the Warp Group Matrix Multiply Accumulate (WGMMA) instruction protocol, which executes collectively across a cooperative warpgroup of 128 threads. This operand asymmetry is fundamentally an instruction set architecture (ISA)-level property. In the underlying &lt;code&gt;wgmma.mma_async&lt;/code&gt; assembly definition, the hardware fixes the matrix row dimension (M) at a constant scale of 64 to define its parallel thread-mapping topology. The column dimension (N) remains highly variable. Operand A strictly maps across this fixed M dimension (using registers or shared memory), and Operand B maps across the variable N dimension (through a shared memory descriptor). These operand slots are therefore structurally non-interchangeable. Consequently, peak hardware tile utilization is governed by these rigid shape encodings, requiring that the M dimension equals or exceeds 64 to prevent compute execution lanes from sitting idle.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This structural preference stems from how Hopper Tensor Cores partition memory blocks. The hardware enforces a fixed minimum layout shape for the leading dimension of the first operand, locking the block height to exactly 64 (M=64). When a mathematical matrix possesses a large, continuous leading dimension in this primary slot, Hopper’s internal hardware schedulers can cleanly map and distribute data across the physical computing grid.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This structural asymmetry becomes a severe performance bottleneck during the decode phase of MoE networks. In a standard MoE layer, the linear algebra governing expert computation is formulated using traditional matrix notation:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;A[M,K] x B[K,N] —&amp;gt; C[M,N]&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Where (M) represents the token row count (or batch size), (K) represents the hidden feature dimension, and (N) represents the expansive expert output dimension.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;During the decode phase, the system processes only one token per user sequence at a time. Consequently, the batch space or token row size is profoundly thin, regularly dropping to a profile where (M &amp;lt; 64), and frequently down to a single row (M = 1). Under this standard structural layout:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Matrix (A) transforms into a thin, fragmented horizontal vector (for example, 1 x 4096).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Matrix (B) (the core expert weights) remains in a large, dense vertical block (for example, 4096 x 8192).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Because the row count (M) is tiny, Matrix (A) is physically incapable of filling the hardware’s fixed (64)-row layout bucket for Operand A. When the WGMMA instruction executes over this layout, it leaves a vast portion of the physical Tensor Core capacity completely starved. The silicon spends clock cycles processing empty spatial slots, leading to poor hardware utilization.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In a standard dense LLM layer, weight matrices are static, unified, and perfectly contiguous. In an MoE layer, however, the workload is fractured across a fleet of independent subnetworks (experts). This creates unique memory and layout bottlenecks that make it hypersensitive to the WGMMA constraints of the Hopper GPU. In a standard dense layer, matrix multiplication multiplies a token vector by a single layer weight matrix. The output dimension (N) is the model’s hidden size (for example, 4096).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In a Fused MoE kernel, the GPU isn’t only multiplying against one expert. It’s grouping and running math for all active experts simultaneously to avoid launching individual kernels for each expert. For a standard dense layer, N is the hidden size (4,096). For a fused MoE layer, N is the number of experts chosen multiplied by the expert intermediate size. For a model running a &lt;code&gt;topk=8&lt;/code&gt; routine, the effective N dimension inside the fused kernel explodes into tens or hundreds of thousands of parameters. Because the N dimension is so disproportionately large compared to the tiny decode token size (M=1), the layout asymmetry becomes extreme. Transposing the matrices moves this large, combined MoE dimension into the primary slot, unlocking hardware prefetching at a scale that standard dense layers cannot match.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To resolve this hardware misalignment without altering the underlying mathematical integrity of the model, we exploit the algebraic property of matrix transposition:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;B&lt;sup&gt;T&lt;/sup&gt;[N,K] x A&lt;sup&gt;T&lt;/sup&gt;[K,M] —&amp;gt; C&lt;sup&gt;T&lt;/sup&gt;[N,M]&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-22058-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/08/ML-22058-1.png" alt="Swap-AB transposition promoting the large expert dimension into the WGMMA operand-A slot for full Tensor Core utilization" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Swap-AB for WGMMA utilization&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;By programmatically flipping the matrix multiplication before it reaches the PTX assembly layer, we realigned the software to match the exact topography of the Hopper silicon. This transposition triggers two immediate architectural advantages on the GPU. First, it saturates the physical layout. The expansive expert dimension (N = 8192) is promoted to the first operand position (B&lt;sup&gt;T&lt;/sup&gt;), and because (N) is large it easily floods the hardware’s mandatory (64)-row threshold. The Tensor Cores then operate at maximum local density with no wasted execution cycles. Second, it activates asynchronous prefetching. Hopper’s dedicated hardware copy engines can prefetch memory addresses asynchronously from shared memory straight into registers before the math execution block fires. Placing the continuous, large weight blocks (B&lt;sup&gt;T&lt;/sup&gt;) in the leading slot lets these engines trace the data layout smoothly and keep the compute pipeline saturated without CPU intervention. This produced a 2–4 percent end-to-end output throughput improvement across large-scale MoE models on H200. We upstreamed this optimization to vLLM, and it is available in version 0.24.0 or later.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="backend-routing-for-fp8-tensor-quantization"&gt;Backend routing for FP8 tensor quantization&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Beyond kernel internals, the choice of which backend executes the MoE computation has a large effect on performance. The vLLM MoE oracle routes different quantization schemes to Triton, FlashInfer+CUTLASS, or other backends. Profiling revealed that for FP8 tensor-quantized models on Hopper, the Triton backend consistently outperforms the default &lt;code&gt;FLASHINFER_CUTLASS&lt;/code&gt; path.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The Triton backend outperforms CUTLASS in low-concurrency MoE workloads primarily because it replaces rigid, framework-level matrix math configurations with custom, operator-level register fusion. CUTLASS relies on standard C++ template structures that treat FP8 tensor scaling, matrix multiplication, and expert aggregation as discrete mathematical operations. The Triton backend instead consolidates this entire pipeline into a single hardware pass. Data is pulled from global high-bandwidth memory (HBM) exactly once and processed entirely within the GPU’s ultra-fast local SRAM registers and compute execution files. It’s then streamed back to the final matrix destination. This removes the slow global VRAM read/write roundtrips that compromise CUTLASS when it is forced to constantly step through intermediate tensor scaling steps.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Furthermore, this register-level fusion is specifically paired with dynamic scheduling layouts designed around variable token bucketing, resolving a severe structural flaw in traditional compilation templates. CUTLASS is natively optimized for dense, large, and predictable matrix dimensions. When it encounters the fluid, ultra-thin batch profiles (M &amp;lt; 64 or M = 1) typical of the live decode generation phase, its default block-tiling and thread-mapping structures become profoundly inefficient. The result is heavy warp under-utilization or expensive memory padding overhead. Conversely, the Triton MoE backend excels at dynamically mapping these small, fragmented token arrays across the physical boundaries of the active expert weights. This keeps the hardware’s internal prefetch streams and Tensor Core pipelines fully saturated even when processing highly unstable workloads at very low batch sizes.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Updating the routing policy is a pure configuration change with no kernel code modified. Still, it yielded a &lt;strong&gt;10 percent end-to-end throughput improvement&lt;/strong&gt; for FP8 quantized fused MoE models on H200. This improvement is available in the AWS Large Model Inference (LMI) v28 container.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="triton-jit-recompilation-elimination"&gt;Triton JIT recompilation elimination&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;One of the most impactful performance pathologies in GPU inference is also one of the least obvious: unnecessary kernel recompilation. Triton, the compiler framework underlying many of vLLM’s custom kernels, specializes integer arguments by default. Every time a kernel is invoked with a previously unseen value for a specialized parameter, Triton triggers a fresh just-in-time (JIT) compilation. In serving workloads where sequence lengths and batch sizes vary continuously, this creates a steady stream of compilation overhead that inflates latency, particularly Time to First Token (TTFT).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In MoE model serving, the &lt;code&gt;_fused_inv_rope_fp8_quant_per_head&lt;/code&gt; kernel (responsible for fused rotary position embedding and FP8 quantization) was recompiling on every new &lt;code&gt;num_tokens&lt;/code&gt; value. Adding &lt;code&gt;do_not_specialize&lt;/code&gt; annotations to the variable-length parameters removed these recompilations entirely, improving TTFT by &lt;strong&gt;9 percent&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;on H200 at 8-way tensor parallelism (PR #42849).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In a multi-GPU cluster, all 8 GPUs must execute their kernels in perfect, microsecond-level synchronization. If even one GPU must stop to recompile its local &lt;code&gt;_fused_inv_rope_fp8_quant_per_head&lt;/code&gt; kernel because its local token batch count changed, the other seven GPUs sit idle and wait for it. By eliminating recompilations across the entire cluster, we smoothed out the multi-GPU coordination overhead, delivering a 9 percent speedup for the user’s initial response latency. The principle here is to treat the compiler as a first-class optimization surface. Understanding how Triton specializes semantics and recognizing when parameters are dead or variable unlocks latency improvements with minimal code change.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;We upstreamed this optimization to vLLM, and it is available in version 0.22.0 or later.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Through our collaboration with the vLLM community, we implemented and open sourced multiple kernel optimizations for fused MoE models. We apply these optimizations in the LMI Deep Learning Container on Amazon SageMaker AI. Together, these optimizations have delivered up to 10 percent higher throughput and up to 9 percent faster TTFT. To get started with custom model hosting, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/deploy-model.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI hosting documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

	<item>
		<title>Managing open-weight model risk in enterprise deployments</title>
		<link>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/managing-open-weight-model-risk-in-enterprise-deployments/</link>
					<comments>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/managing-open-weight-model-risk-in-enterprise-deployments/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Michael Scovetta]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 16 Oct 2026 16:05:00 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[AI + machine learning]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/?p=54665</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>The growing AI landscape is giving enterprises more choice in how they build, customize, and deploy increasingly capable models.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/managing-open-weight-model-risk-in-enterprise-deployments/">Managing open-weight model risk in enterprise deployments</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The growing AI landscape is giving enterprises more choice in how they build, customize, and deploy increasingly capable models. Open-weight models are an important part of that landscape. They give enterprises access to model weights and greater flexibility to inspect, evaluate, modify, and run models within their own environments.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">While that flexibility can be valuable, it also affects how enterprises evaluate models, where they implement safeguards, and who is responsible for maintaining those safeguards over time.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">As enterprises choose which models to deploy, the relevant question is broader than whether a model is open-weight or closed-weight. Enterprises need to understand what they are using the model for, what could go wrong in that context, and what evidence and controls they need to manage risks.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Today, Microsoft and NVIDIA are publishing Risk Management for Open-Weight Models in Enterprise, the first in a planned series of joint resources intended to help enterprises address those questions. The paper introduces a shared risk taxonomy and an approach to managing model risks grounded in established standards and frameworks, including International Organization for Standardization (ISO) standards and the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) AI Risk Management Framework.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The paper reflects a core idea: weight openness alone does not determine an AI system’s risk. Risk depends on the model, use case, deployment, and safeguards.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Read the whitepaper: Risk Management for Open-Weight Models in Enterprise<br>Understanding risk in the deployment context<br>Open- and closed-weight models can share many of the same underlying risks, but how those risks materialize depends on the deployment. A model used to help an employee draft a document presents different considerations than the same model used in a customer-facing workflow, just as a standalone inference endpoint differs from a system connected to external data or tools.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The architecture matters too. A risk may originate in the model, while the most effective control may sit in the inference environment, application, or surrounding operational processes. The joint guidance looks beyond the model label to factors including provenance, intended use, users, deployment conditions, and surrounding controls. This first paper focuses primarily on model-layer risks. Evaluating a model alone does not establish the safety of an agent or application: teams must also assess its permissions, data access, tools, runtime, and operational controls.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Establishing a common language<br>Managing these risks consistently also requires a common vocabulary across engineering, security, legal, safety, and business teams.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The initial Microsoft and NVIDIA taxonomy organizes risks into five broad categories: Cybersecurity, Data Protection, Content, Human-AI Interaction, and Bias. It is not a policy or deployment checklist, but instead it should be used by enterprises to adapt it to their own systems, use cases, and risk criteria, then connect relevant risks to evaluations, mitigations, and accountable owners.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Using evaluations as risk evidence<br>Evaluations help turn the taxonomy into something enterprises can use. The paper maps evaluations to a selection of risks across the five categories, giving teams a way to start with a risk that matters for their deployment and identify evaluations that can produce relevant evidence about it.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Microsoft Foundry helps enterprises assess quality, groundedness, and safety with built-in and custom evaluations and automated red teaming. Its configurable content-safety controls help detect and mitigate harmful inputs and outputs and prompt attacks. Teams should validate control availability for their model and deployment, including preview status, and combine these capabilities with application-specific testing, monitoring, and human oversight.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Evaluations still need to be interpreted in the context of a particular deployment and the goal is to make it easier for teams to connect the risks they identify with useful evidence, while building a broader set of evaluation resources that can evolve alongside models, deployment patterns, and emerging risks. Microsoft and NVIDIA plan to use the joint repository to share additional evaluations and point to existing resources that can help enterprises assess specific risks.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Accounting for changes in control and responsibility<br>Open-weight access gives enterprises greater control over how a model is evaluated, modified, and deployed. Model access and deployment are separate choices: open-weight models can also be used through managed platforms. Responsibility for safeguards depends on the service and deployment configuration; enterprises that operate their own model deployments may take on controls otherwise provided by a managed service.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Those safeguards can operate at different layers, and (depending on the risk) enterprises might use model changes, access controls, input and output filtering, monitoring, or human review. The important step is connecting each identified risk to controls that can effectively address it within the deployed system. Those decisions need to be revisited as models and deployments change.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Building on this work<br>Risk Management for Open-Weight Models in Enterprise focuses primarily on the model layer. Future work from Microsoft and NVIDIA is intended to extend this approach across the runtime, harness, and application layers, with additional technical guidance on evaluation and mitigation.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">We also expect the resources supporting this work to evolve as new models, evaluations, and deployment patterns emerge. The joint repository will provide a place to share additional evaluation resources, including contributions from the broader community.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">For enterprises deploying open-weight models today, the paper provides guidance they can begin applying to their own use cases. We encourage developers, security practitioners, researchers, and others working in this space to read the paper, explore the evaluations, and contribute evaluations that can help strengthen this shared body of work.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Read the joint Microsoft-NVIDIA paper, Risk Management for Open-Weight Models in Enterprise.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Explore the joint repository and technical resources.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Risk Management for Open-Weight Models in Enterprise was developed jointly by Microsoft and NVIDIA, with Andrew Berkley and April Chen of Microsoft and Michael Boone, Eileen Long, and Arun Rao of NVIDIA leading the development and editing of the paper.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Explore Microsoft Foundry evaluations and content-safety controls for implementation guidance.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">To learn more about Microsoft Security solutions, visit our website. Bookmark the Security blog to keep up with our expert coverage on security matters. Also, follow us on LinkedIn (Microsoft Security) and X (@MSFTSecurity) for the latest news and updates on cybersecurity.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/managing-open-weight-model-risk-in-enterprise-deployments/">Managing open-weight model risk in enterprise deployments</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>
